Bond market weak. Bankers apppehensive ability market absorb government
military bonds. Reported Deposits Bureau accepted yen 63,000,000 and Bank

Government insisting banks adopt more lenient attitude toward borrowers

Increase currency circulat ion seems certain and imminent.
Country bank dif ficulties becoming more apparent - will hasten mergers.
Unemployment unrelieved. Retail prices continue downward despite declining
exchange.
General imports seriously affected by depreciated exchange. Exports continue
low. Trade with Central China shows little improvement despite easing situation.

SECTION K - BANK LOANS
50
At the end of 1930, the last year for which Govern-
9
9
ment statistics are available, the Commercial Attache
reports a total of 9,458,000,000 yen in bank loans
Note
outstanding. These are long term and not commercial
894.51
loans. This total has heen foreign renewals since 1927,
when it stood at 9,600,000,000 yen indicating very little
liquidation.
Land mortgage loans amount to 2,870,000,000. Condi-
tions in agricultural regions are bad and operations (?)
real estate projects are not profitable. Many competent
observers are of the opinion that the present worth of
the security for these loans is not more than one-third
of the value at the time these loans were made. If
liquidation were attempted now many banks would fail.
Industrial loans amount to 527,000,000 yen and
show a steady increase over several years. Well in-
formed opinion infers a large part of this money cannot be
liquidated and will have to be written off.
Commodity

The Address of Mr. Yoshizawa
Minister for
Foreign Affairs, at the 6lst Session
of the Imperia? Diet.
March 22nd, 1932.
At the last session of the Diet I had occasion to
discuss the more important of the foreign questions
confronting this country. To-day I have the pleasure
of reporting, and of stating my views, on the various
phases that have since developed.
As regards the Anti-Japanese movement in China Proper,
the Japanese Government seized every opportunity to urge
upon the Chinese authorities the thorough-going suppression
of the same. However, China failed to respond, and the
movement continued with increasing intensity and persistence.
In Shanghai, where the agitation was conducted with especial
vigor, the Minkuo Jihpao, a vernacular journal of that city.
published on Ja mary 9th an article highly abusive of our
Imperial House, and on the 18th of the same month Japanese
his
priests and tholr companions were murdered or wounded by
a Chinese mob. The two incidents were sufficient to inflame
the Japanese residents, who had long endured the ceaseless
Anti-Japanese movements and whose patience had been well-nigh
exhausted under the trying conditions of more recent months.
The situation thus came to assume a grave aspect.
The

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
The Japanese Consul-General at Shanghai, acting under
the instructions of the Government, presented to the Mayor
of Shanghai strong protests regarding these two incidents,
and at the same time made various demands for the settlement
of the affairs. The Mayor complied without much demur with
our demands relating to the first incident, and after some
delay and much conversation, in the end accepted our terms
regarding the second. On receipt of the Mayor's reply, at
3 o'clock on the 28th, the Japanese authorities, anticipating
that the situation would improve. adopted an attitude of
watchfully awaiting the fulfilment of their promise on the
part of the Chinese. In the meantime, the 19th Route Army.
then concentrated in the vicinity of Shanghai, having assumed
a disquieting attitude and making a display of warlike
preparations, the Shanghai Municipal Council had decided
to take due precautions against the possibility of
disturbances being created within the Settlement by
undisciplined Chinese soldiers, or by mobs and radical
elements. Accordingly, the Council, meeting on the 28th.
declared a state of siege, to commence that day at 4
o'clock in the afternoon. As a result, the garrisons
of the Powers were assigned to duty at their respective
sectors in accordance with a pre-arranged plan of common
defense. It was while the Japanese marines were proceeding
to take up their post in the Chapei district, their allotted
sector, that the Chinese fired upon our forces, and compelled
them

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
 hostilities, but the matter was already pending in the
hands of the Council under Article 1l of the Covenant and,
moreover, the commission of inquiry had indeed left for the
purpose of conducting investigations on the spot into the
actual conditions prevailing in China; there was therefore
no legitimate ground for applying Article 15 to either of
these cases. It was with an unequivocal reservation based
on that view that our government participated in the delibera-
tions of the Council and the Assembly. However, the Assembly
passed a resolution on March 1lth, which was in more than
one respect unacceptable to our government. On account of
this unacceptability, our representatives under the instruc-
tions of the Government, expounded the Japanese position,
made a reservation
doclared a protest embodying our contention with regard to
the application of Article 15 of the Coverant, and abstained
from voting.
In Manchuria, by reason of the special position it
occupies in relation to China Proper, and the inveterate
antagonism of the Manchurian people towards the militarist
rule to which they had been subjected, it appears that
subsequently to the downfall of the erstwhile Manchurian
authorities last autumn, the efforts of the local leaders
for the maintenance of peace and order in their respective
districts gradually assumed the form of a movement for
autonomy. A decl ration of independence was made a short
time

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
In the foreign affairs of Jap:n. While the interests of
this country in Manchuria are certainly of paramount impor-
tance from a political view-point, those in China Proper are
in the main eoonomic rather than political. Consequently, if
the Nanking Government and the Nationalist Party abandon
their Anti-Japanese policy and direct their energies towards
achieving internal unity and peace, and economic prosperity
and progress, there is no room for doubt as to the possibility
of restoring complete friendship and concord botween the two
countries. I may add on this occasion a word about the
atmosphere of Europe and America, which heas not always been
sympathetie. to Japan since the outbreak of the Manchurian
Incident, and especially since the Shanghai Affair. That
atmosphere was created simply through misrepresentations of
facts emanatin from Chinese sources or through misunder-
standings of various kinds. I am confident that in time
the sentiment of the Western Powers will turn gradually in
favor of Japan.

interest:

The South Manohuria Railway sterling loan, which

the existence of any such loan. They both ridiculed the
possibility both as matter of business and politics.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 28, 1932.
Revised Working Budget.
The Embassy (in despatch No. 553,
April 5, 1932) gives details of the work-
ing budget of Japan for 1932-33. It is
pointed out that the budget fails to
balance by 69,000,000 yen. Moreover,
the extraordinary revenue to be applied
to estimated expenditure includes some
90,000,000 yen to be raised by loans.
It is believed that even this figure for
loans is under-estimated and that a great
deal more money will have to be borrowed
to finanoe the Empire's undertakings.
The figures indicate retrenchment in
expenditure, but this is misleading, the
budget having been inherited from the
Minseito. The Government has no idea of
pursuing a deflation policy.
WRL:EJL

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
press.
This shows a reduction in expenditures of Yen
34,136,000 and a decrease in revenue of Yen 103,055,000.
(Actually, however, the Minseito appropriations have been
out by Yen 93,655,000, for the working budget, as recently
published, includes the supplementary budget of Yen 59,519,-
oo0 for the present fiscal year*, which was passed at the
recent extraordinary session of the Diet for expenditures
arising from the Manchurian and Shanghai expeditions from
April 1 of this year.) Despite this reduction in expendi-
tures, however, the budget fails to balance by Yen 68,548,000.
In addition to this fact, the extraordinary revenue appears
to be in large part made up of loans. The following table
illustrates reductions and increases in somewhat more detail:

Party claims that the Government has done no more than
alter the name of and reduce appropriations which by
the Minseito Cabinet were designated as for relief of
unemployment and estimated at Yen 39,700,000 for the De-
partment of Home Affairs, and Yen 4,700,000 for the De-
partment of Agriculture and Forestry.
The effects of
the recent assassinations and plotted assassinations
(despatch No. 561 of April 7, 1932) and the alleged
. spread of extreme thoughts, both reactionary and radical,
are reflected in an appropriation of Yen 90,O00 under the
Department of Home Affairs for the control of firearms
and gunpowder, an appropriation of Yen 102,000 under the
Department of Justice for the prevention of crimes arising
from "new thoughts", and an appropriation of Yen 206,000
under the Department of Education "for the improvement of
thought", the latter being an increase of Yen 144,000 over
the parallel appropriation in the Minseito budget.
An
appropriation of Yen 35,000 under the Department of Com-
munications "for the examination of foreign mail" is ap-
parently intended to prevent entry into the country of
so-called dangerous thoughts in printed form and is be-
lieved to be the first appearance in a Japanese budget of
this item. In the Department of Commerce and Industry
the items of interest are Yen 44l,000 for the encourage-
ment of the manufacture of iron, discussed on page ll of
despatch No. 578 of April 19, 1932, Yen 100,000 for the
exploitation of oil resources in North Saghalien, Yen 130,-
ooo for the encouragement of the manufacture of motor cars,
and Yen 143,000 and Yen 32,000 for economic investigations
in Manchuria and Mongolia.

described

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)

In the ordinary supplementary revenue the items of
customs duties and Bank of Japan profits are worthy of
note.
By a bill passed on June 14, 1932, the specific
duties in the Japanese Customs Tariff were raised by a
straight 35 per cent, in order to compensate for the de-
creased value of the yen.
There were also other in-
creases in the rates, both ad valorem and specific, of
conmodities, such as pig iron, wheat, petroleum products
and timber, to which it was desired to extend further
protection.
These increased customs duties will, it is
estimated, bring in the additional customs revenue listed.
The share of the profits of the Bank of Japan, included in
the supplementary revenue, is also the result of a bill
passed by the Diet on June 14, 1932.
Under this act a
share of the profits earned by the Bank because of the
excess fiduciary note issue of Yen 880,000,000 just author-
ized, accrues to the Government. In
In the extraordinary supplementary revenue, the only
item of importance is that of loans.
It will be noted
that the loans amount to Yen 364,540,000 (including frac-
tions).
The bulk of these loans are to provide for ex-
penditures included in the supplementary budget, but Yen
100,700,000 is for the purpose of covering the deficit in
the ordinary or working budget.
The matter of loans
will be discussed later in this report.

budget are as follows:

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
A.b
The Supplementary Budget, which passed the Diet on
June 14, 1932, was notable in that it provided for a
total of Yen l82,956,000 for estimated expenditures from
Jume 1, 1932, to January 31, 1933, in connection with the
Nanchuria and Shanghai affairs, and for bond issues of a
tetal of Yen 364,540,000 to cover deficits in the working
budget and the supplementary budget. The appropriations
to eover the estimated expenditures in the Manohuria and
Shanghai affairs were distributed as follows: Yon 5,53l,-
000 to the Foreign Ofrice; Yon 20,000,000 to the Finance
Department as a first reserve; Yen 117,715,000 to the war
Department: and Ten 39.710,000 to the Navy Department.
The bond issues authorized consisted of road construotion
loans, Yen 14,467,000; Manchuria affair loans, Ten 189,-
526,000; and defic1t covoring loan, Yen 160,546,000.
Appropriations amounting to Yen 70,757,000 wero also inoluded
in the supplementary budget for measures intonded to relieve
the economic depression*.

Budget Bills.
The total expenditures proposed by the
Government amount to about Yen 175,000,000, which will
almost entirely be raised by loans to be taken up by the
Bank of.Japan. Of this amount about Yen 163,000,000 are
included in the general account, while Yen 12,500,000 are
included in various special accounts. In addition, it is
also proposed that the various prefectures expend Yen
87,000,000 for miscellaneous relief purposes, bringing
the total expenditures of both central and local govern-
ments to Yen 263,000,000 as additional relief measures
during the present fiscal year ending March 31, 1933. The
following table shows the proposals in broad outline:

The exact use to which the several ministries will
put the money placed at their disposal has not been
officially announced as yet, and the only indications of
that nature available to the Embassy have been scattered
statements in the vernacular press.
The different state-
ments vary, but not in any substantial amount. In general,
it appears that the expenditures will more or less follow
the lines indicated below:
The Foreign Office will expend its money for "the
control of Japanese residents abroad", which has not been
explained.
The Department of Home Affairs
will spend 48 million
of its total of almost 6l million yen on public works, such
as road building and the like, for the relief of agrarian
villages. Other items are relief of peasants in the
Hokkaido, medical relief, provisions for the unemployed,
and the like.
The Department of Finance will spend its allotment
on interest on loans, improvements on Customs Houses, and
the like.
The Army Department will spend most of its portion
in manufacturing munitions. Small amounts are earmarked
for improvements in arsenals and airfields. It is stated
that the purpose of this expenditure is to give aid to
private manufacturers by placing orders
with
them.
This would also grant employment to many laborers.
The Navy Department
will construct new ships of
various kinds, as well as perfect its air organization.
The

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
With regard to the loans, totalling about 12-1/2
million yen, to Chosen, Taiwan and Karafuto, the bulk
of the expenditures will be on riparian works, harbors,
and similar public undertakings.
The chief feature of these relief expenditures under
the budget is that for the most part they are for the
construction of public works of all kinds. Aid in the form
of small interest loans to farmers, and similar items of
a credit nature to relieve financial distress among rural
populations and the smaller businessmen and industrialists,
is included in measures not carried out under the budget,
but are extra-budgetary loans from government agencies
to the parties cnncerned. They are discussed later.
Loans and the Issue of Currency
As indicated in the figures quoted in the earlier
sections of this report, the loans to be floated under the
provisions of this proposed supplementary budget amount
to Yen 175,441,000. It was reported in the Embassy's
despatch No. 20 of June 17, 1932, that the loans provided
for up to that time for the current fiscal year amounted
to Yen 529,500,000. The present loans, if passed, will
therefore bring the total amount floated during the year
to Yen 704,941,888, including those for the Manchurian and
Shanghai affairs.
The loans included in this supplementary budget, when
added to the revenue, both ordinary and extraordinary, bring
the supplementary budget total to Yen 175,056,728. When
this sum is added to the working and supplementary budget
already

It is understood that the funds thus made available for
relief enterprises will be issued by the Deposits Bureau
at a low rate of interest, and will be backed and guaranteed
by the State, which thus becomes responsible for an even
greater sum than that contemplated in the regular budget.
Many think that these measures, while ostensibly loans
made by the Deposits Bureau and which are theoretically
to be repaid, will never be so repaid, and that the State
will in the end have to bear this burden also.
It should be noted that here, as elsewhere in this
report, no figures have been officially made public as yet,
and the Embassy cannot guarantee their accuracy, although
there is little doubt as to their general trend.

Possible Opposition in the Diet.
The Government, naturally enough, asserts that it will
experience little or no difficulty in getting its measures
approved by the lower house.
There are many indications,
nevertheless, pointing to the possibility of there being
strong and perhaps violent opposition to its proposals.
Although the present Saito Cabinet is a coalition one, it
is generally conceded that its fiscal measures are largely
Minseito in tendency, although Finance Minister Takahashi
is himself a prominent member of the Seiyukai. Many of the
younger members of this latter party have in the last fort-
night or so come out strongly against the Government's
proposals, and have reportedly gone so far as to try to
influence Takahashi in their favor.
Apparently he has remained
firm in his attitude of approval, and in cooperation with the
president of the party, Dr. Suzuki, it is evident that he has
endeavored to  lacate these younger though influential
oppositionists, with but little success according to the press.
Nothing of course is known as to what will happen in the
Diet if these malcontents decide to
attempt drastic measures,
but the Premier has gone so far as to say that he is ready
for dissolution if such a step becomes necessary.
The Kokumin Domei, Adachi's new party with about thirty
seats in the llouse, hes come out strongly against the Cabinet.
and may try to
cause the Government's downfall.
Inasmuch
as these thirty seats have been acquired at the expense of
the Minseito, and also because the Seiyukai commands a large
majority, anything is possible if the opposition movement
becomes strong, even though there be no alliance between these
two factions.
The

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
parliamentary government is facing a crisis; consequently,
despite undoubted opposition, it is not unlikely that the
House will, as in June, refrain from the usual violent
and bitterly partisan attacks for which it is notorious.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

The meaning of the various portions of these estimates
for the special accounts are not clear in all respects. For
instance, expenditures under the National Loan include sums
for Chosen, Taiwan, and Karafuto. Expenditures for these
same areas are also mentioned in expenditures controlled by
the Ministry of Overseas Affairs. Although it is not so
stated, the Embassy interprets these latter expenditures to be
loans of the Government General concerned, as distinguished
from national loans, and also that they are to be included in
the 87,000,000 yen provincial expenditures mentioned on page
2 of the Embassy's despatch No. 105 of August 25, 1932.
Respectfully yours,

whether in greater or less degree.
The principal factors in the economic situation
in Japan today are the very heavy indebtedness of the
farmers, small industrialists and merchants, plans
for the relief of such indebtedness, the size of the
national debt, fiscal legislation providing for bond
issues to cover the relief plans and the expenditures
for the military adventures on the Asiatic ainland,
and the depreciation of the currency.
Indebtedness of Farmers, Small Industrialists, and
Merchants.
The extent of the indebtedness of the farmers of
Japan is not known exactly but estimates place it at
from Yen 5,000,000,000 to Yen 6,000,000,000. The fall
in the price of rice, raw silk and other products of
Japanese agriculturists during 1930 and 1931 rendered
it impossible for the farmers even to pay interest on
their debts, while the fall in the value of farm lands
would have bankrupted the country banks had they
foreclosed on the farms held as security for loans.
Agrarian unrest, arising from these difficulties,
caused the Government to consider seriously the
necessity of relieving the farmers. In addition, the
small industrialists and merchants are likewise hope-
lessly in debt. Their debts were estimated by Mr.
Nakajima, Minister of Commerce and Industry, in reply
to an interpellation in the Diet, at Yen 1,600,000,000.
It appears, therefore, that the indebtedness for which
relief

the undertaking of public works to give extra employ-
ment to the farmers.
The actual plans worked out by the Cabinet
and submitted to the present extraordinary session
of the Diet for approval combine many of the proposals
mentioned above. It is proposed to use around
Yen 1o0,0o0,o00 for public works, such as road-building,
riparian works, public buildings, et cetera. The
Army and Navy wish to use Yen 37,000,000 for purchase
of supplies(as far as possible from small and medium
merchants) and for labor in working the supplies into
military and naval equipment. It is proposed to use
some Yen 12,000,000 to assist destitute communities
in maintaining educational facilities.. The total
national budget for direct relief measures during the
present fiscal year is Yen 175,000,000. In addition
the prefectures are to expend Yen 87,000,000 for relief
in the form of public orks. Direct relief to destitute
families is to be rendered by using the Natural Disaster
Relief Fund.
In addition to the direct expenditures by the
national and prefectural governments, relief is to be
extended by authorizing various governmental financial
institutions, such as the Deposits Bureau and the
Hypothec Bank, to readjust loans to farmers and to
relieve country banks of frozen assets. Payment of
certain

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
is claimed that the Government cannot afford larger
direct expenditures at the present time. However,local
economic conditions have recently changed to the farmers'
and industrialists' advantage, rendering it less imperative
to take drastic steps to relieve them. The heavy fall in
the value of the yen and the rise in prices in the United
States have caused a general rise in prices of agri-
cultural products and, to a less extent, of industrial
products, within Japan, and consequently Japanese debtors
are no longer in as desperate circumstances as they were
six months ago.
The funds for direct national relief, amounting
to Yen 175,000,000, are to be raised by bond issues.
The Japanese National Debt.
The bond issues provided for in the bills now before
the Diet, added to those already authorized, bring the
total of national borrowing in the fiscal year ending
March 31, 1933, to over Yen 700,000,000, of which only
a small amount is for refunding maturing loans. These
bond issues will add a considerable sum to Japan's
national debt, and it would be well here briefly to
review the history of the national debt during the past
ten years.
The national debt of Japan, internal and external,
according to the statistics of the Department of Finance,
at the close of each fiscal year from 1923 to 1932 has
been as follows:

Japan, therefore, is not bearing an undue burden of
debt- in fact, it is one of the most fortunate countries
in the world in this respect. The only disquieting
feature in connection with the national debt of Japan
is the constant increase, despite the period of com-
parative prosperity from 1921 to 1929. There seemed
to be little or no inclination during this period to
balance the national budgets and recourse was had annually
to borrowing.
During the 1932-33 fiscal year, because of the
necessity of raising funds for the Japanese military
campaigns in China and for agrarian and industrial
relief, the national debt will be increased by some
Yen 700,000,000, which will bring the total debt to
nearly Yen 7,000,000,000.
Depreciation of Currency.
The opinion has been gaining strength in Japan
that the currency should be depreciated, following the
example of some of the European countries. This is
advocated, not to relieve the Government of a part of
its internal indebtedness(because the internal debt is
not unduly large), but primarily to relieve the farming
class of a part of its indebtedness, to assist the
exporters
2

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
security of bonds without payment of tax to Yen 1,000,000,000.
This permitted the Bank to take up additional bonds to
the amount of Yen 880,000,000, or somewnat in excess of
the proposed issues for the year.
When the Bank buys Government bonds, credits are
established against which the governmental departments
may draw. A part of the credits remains in the banks
but a part can be expected to go into circulation as
currency. Some inflation of the currency could therefore
be expected, and with inflation there would undoubtedly
come a lower foreign exclange value for the yen and a
tendency for capital to flow from Japan.
3. In order to prevent the flight of capital from
Japan on inflation of the currency, a law was passed giving
the Government authority to prohibit or restrict the
purchase of foreign currencies and foreign exchange bills,
transactions in foreign currencies, and the purchase or
sale of foreign-currency securities, as well as to re-
quire all persons holding foreign currencies, foreign
currency credits or foreign securities to report them
to the Government. The Government may also require those
holding foreign currencies, foreign credits or foreign-
currency securities to sell them to the Bank of Japan.
These measures, it was believed, would effectually pre-
vent such a flight of capital as occurred in the autumn
of 1931.
To the time of writing(September 8, 1932), there
has

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
keep their foreign credits abroad as long as may
: be possible, in order to gain by further falls in the
yen.
This fall in the exchange value of the yen has been
of considerable temporary benefit to the country and
has done much to relieve distressed industrialists and
merchants for the time being. Domestic prices naturally
tend to rise much more slowly than the yen falls, and
consequently concerns manufacturing for export and export
merchants find themselves in a very advantageous position.
Raw silk, as an example, has more than doubled in its
yen price within the past three months, thus benefiting
the farmer, the reeler, the exporter and the banks
holding silk paper. Exports have increased appreciably
and imports have decreased, resulting in a favorable
balance of trade in the past month. Domestic prices
have slowly begun to rise, creating activity in local
markets.
Summary.
The internal economic situation in Japan has
improved appreciably in the last two months. In the
early summer the position of the farmers appeared
to be desperate and drastic relief measures were urged.
At the present time, with the firmness of the rice
market and the very pronounced rise in the silk market,
and with measures authorized which will relieve the
cases of extreme need, the agrarian community can be
considered to have temporarily tided over the crisis.
The

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
The falling exchange value of the yen has assisted
manufacturers and exporters and has caused a rising
tendency in domestic prices. Large stocks of very
cheap raw materials purchased before the decline of
the yen are being held by importers and mills.
There
is undoubtedly a vast number of industrial concerns and
mercantile houses which are insolvent and which are
being bolstered up by the banks in hopes of better
times, but this burden and menace has been carried for
several years now, and there is no indication that the
condition is appreciably worse than it has been at any
time since 1927. There is some unemployment(estimated
at around 500,00o) but the Japanese system of discharge
allowances and the practice of laborers in hard times
of returning to the farms from which they came tend to
prevent any serious problem of unemployment.
While internally the country is in a reasonably
good condition, externally Japan is faced with economic
difficulties which may depress its credit still further.
The country must find some fifty or sixty million dollars
each year to meet the interest on its external obligations,
while there is an average excess of im orts of around
$80,000,000.
These external payments have been met in
the past by borrowings abroad and by the invisible excess
of exports(profits from undertakings abroad, steamship
earnings, remittances from emigrants, et cetera). It
now appears, from reports vhich have reached Tokyo, that
Japan is unable to raise further loans abroad, while the
invisible excess of exports has greatly decreased, if it
has

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
-17 -
330,000,000. These were covered by bond issues. If
expenditures are continued at this low rate, and if
warlike fervor is maintained, the nation should be
able to finance the campaign for two or three years
to come. The Bank of Japan can still absorb ad-
ditional bonds covering over another year's extra-
ordinary military expenditure; after that other means
of financing must be found. If patriotic fervor is
maintained, it is probable that another year can be
financed by public subscription .
(b).
Economic Boycott Against Japan.
Japan is practically self-sustaining as regards
food, clothing, copper, chemicals and various other essen-
tials, and with control of Manchuria, would be self-sus-
taining in regard to iron and coal. Certain raw materials,
such as rubber, petroleum, tin, zinc, aluminum, cotton and
wool are imported, but it is believed that sufficient
stocks are being held to carry the country for some time
if strict rationing is adopted. Students of the situation
believe that Japan, if cut off from intercourse with the
outside world, could sustain itself for a period of at
least five years with ractically unimpaired military
strength. Whether or not the people, accustomed as they
are to a rising standard of living, would submit to the
imposition upon them of the much lower standard made
necessary by rationing, cannot be predicted.
An

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
An economic boycott against Japan, therefore,
would not be likely to be successful unless the people
rose in revolt against those who brought the boycott
upon them, or unless the manufacturing, mercantile and
banking classes used pressure to end the conditions causing
the boycott.
(c). Financial Pressure Against Japan.
As was pointed out before in this report,
Japan is not in an advantageous position in regard to its
external economic relations. It is having difficulty in
finding sufficient funds to meet its external obligations,
and it is probable that this difficulty will increase
rather than diminish in the future. Japan's credit abroad
is not good(Japanese Government bonds are selling in New
York at a discount of from 37 to 47 per cent) and it is
unlikely that further foreign loans can be negotiated for
some years to come. With customs barriers be ing erected
against Japanese goods in many of her est markets, with
the Chinese boycott again becoming active, and with the
possibility of voluntary,unofficial boycotts in other
countries, there is little hope that Japan's export trade
will improve sufficiently to pay for her annual interest and
amortization obligations.
The indications therefore are that continued financial
pressure on Japan from abroad will in time destroy the
chauvinistic

Tokyo.
Circulaire.
Ces deniers temps, plusieurs Ambassades et Légations
à Tokio ont bien voulu demander au Ministere Impérial
des Affaires étrangères des renseignements sur l'applica-
tion de la Loi pour empecher la fuite des capitaux (loi
No. 17), ainsi que sur celle des arretés du Ministère
des Finances No. 12 et No. 13, publiés le ler juillet
dernier (voir Journal Officiel du ler juillet).
Le Ministère des Finances, à qui le Ministère des
Affaires étrangères s'est adressé pour prendre son avis,
vient de lui faire savoir qu'étant donné l'esprit de
l'élaboration de ces loi et arretés, il serait désirable
de prier même Messieurs les membres du Corps diplomatique
de remplir les formalités prévues par lesdits loi et ar-
retés et par conséquent de présenter, quant à leurs dépots
en monnaie étrangère, des demandes d'autorisation, par
l'intermédiaire de leurs banques, conformément aur formali-
tés prévues à l'article 4 de l'arrêté No. 13( ci-joint la
traduction provisoire).
Le Ministere des Affaires etrangères a donc l'honneur
de porter à la connaissance des Ambassades et Légations à
Tokio ce qui précède en y ajoutant que, d'après lui, lorsque
les demandes parviendront, le Ministère des Finance les
examinera avec toute bienveillance possible et prendra des
mesures nécessaires pour ne pas créer pratiquement d'ennuis.
Tokio, le 25 aout 1932.

Despatch No. 118

From American Embasay
Tokyo.
Circular
Several Embassies and Legations have recently been
asking the Imperial Ministry for Foreign Affairs for in-
formation concerning the appliaation of the Law to Pre-
vent the Flight of Capital (Law No. 17) as well as of
the decrees No. 12 and 13 of the Ministry of Finance, pub-
lished on July lst (see Official Gazette for July 1).
The Finance Ministry, having been asked by the Ministry
for Foreign Affairs to give its opinion, has just stated
that, following the intention of the law and decrees, it
it would seem proper to request the members of the diplo-
matic corps to fulfill the formalities prescribed by the
aforesaid law and decrees. Therefore, as regards their
accounts in foreign currency, they should present applica-
tions through their banks, in the manner prescribed by
Article 4 of Decree No. 13 (a provisional translation is
attached).
The Ministry of Foreign Affairs accordingly has the
honor to convey the above information to the Embassies and
Legations at Tokyo with the additional assurance from the
Ministry of Finance that when the applications are submitted
it will examine them with all possible good will and will
take steps to avoid creating more difficulties than are
necessary.
Tokyo, August 26,1932.

=== Page 120 === (confidence 1.00)
Article 4 of Decree No. 13 of the Ministry of Finance
(Provisional Translation)
Those who desire to obtain authorization to have
a bank account in foreign money, or to lend or borrow
foreign money, are required to forward to the Minister
of Finance an application containing the following in-
formation:
1. Domicile, profession, name and surname (or firm's
name) of the applicant.
2. The maximum balance of the account, or the amount
to be loaned or borrowed.
3. The nature and conditions of the account or loan.
4. Place of the contract.
5. Domicile, profession, name and surname (or firm's
name)of the other party to the contract.
6. If the contract is made for the account of another
party, the domicile, profession, name and surname
(or firm's name) of that party.
7.The date prescribed for opening the account, or the
term prescribed for the loan.
8. The object of the contract and the various circum-
stances which have necessitated it.
9. Other information.

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.98)
-17 -
III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS
Extraordinary Session of the Diet.
(a) Japanese internal political activities during
the month of August were contered on the preparations for
the extraordinary session of the Diet which convened on the
twenty-second. The session was called to consider plans
for rural relief. The Government had great difficulty in
deciding upon its bill, there being a wide divergence between
the desires of the Government departments who do the spending
and the views of the Minister of Finance who hes to find
the money. As far as the Government bill was concerned
the latter had his way to as great an extent as could be
hoped for. but when the various provisions of the bill
became known to the public there was a tremendous stir
among the politicians. The Government advanced a three-year
program for relief, involving an expenditure of Yen 1,655,000,000
of which Yen 222,500, 000 is provided for in the present re-
11ef b111 and Yen 175,000,000 in the supplementary budget .
The amount of this relief was declared to be inadequste
and various groups of Diet mambers announced their op-
position and their determination to see that more relief
was provided. The new party formed by Mr. Adachi and
calling itself the Kokumin Domei, or National League, ex-
pressed 1ts determination to force the Government to provide
more. Much more serious was the opposition that developed
among the mambers of the Seiyukai, the majority party in
the Diet. One faction, numbering more than a hundred
threatened to join the National League, which contains about
thirty members, and force a dissolution of the Government.
It

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.98)
- 24 -
GOVERNMENT FINANCE *
The Yem 175,441,000 exéenditure for relief provided
for in the Govermment's budget bill, which 1s before the
presemt session of the Diet, is to be raised by an internal
lean which will be taken up almost entirely by the Bank of
Japan. This will bring the total amount of the Government
loans floated during the year to Yen 704,941,888. The
outlays provided for in the bill will bring the total
budget expenditures for the year to the unprecedented sum
of Yen 1,955,441,000 as against a normal outlay of
Y 1,350.000.000.
The other relief measures before the Diet provide that
during the present fiscal year the Deposits Hureau will make
ava1lable through 1oans about Yen 222,500,000. Although
these are theoretieally to be repaid, it is thought by
many that the Government will eventually have to bear this
burden also. The loans to be advanced by the Government
through the Deposits Bureau during the course of the three-
year rel1ef program amount to Yen 900,000,000. They are
for liquidation of frozen real estate, relief of smaller
industrial end commercial enterprises, improved financing
of industrial unions, and readjustment of farmers' debts.

yen. The estimated national revenue
for 1933 is 1,250,000,000 yen. The
deficit for next year will amount to
over a billion yen which must be met
by bond issues or tax inoreases.

may possibly be increased.

Sir:
The public in Japan is at present seriously concer ed
FILFD
over the new claims submitted by the various Ministries
against next year's budget. Notwithstanding the severe
economic depression affecting the country, the Ministries
have presented new claims amounting to over Yen 1,200,000,000.
Added to the ordinary estimate of expenditures(see table
following) this figure would bring the total of next year's
budget to the unprecedented sum of approximately
Yen 2,500,000,000.

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
These figures show graphically the enormous
new burdens which the nation has assumed during the
past year and will assume next year if present plans
are carried through. At the same time comparison of
the two columns shows how completely all previous plans
of finance have been upset by the present demands.
The estimated expenditure for the coming year is
arrived at by adding the new claims to the proper figure
of the budget program. These new claims are conrmonly
believed to be reducible from the Yen 1,200,000,000
demanded to not less than Yen 1,000,000,000. Hence,
this figure added to Yen 1,521,865,000 equals approxi-
mately Yen 2,500,000,000. Contrasting with this figure
is the estimated national revenue for 1933, of Yen
1,250,000,000.* The deficit for next year alone will
amount to over a billion yen which must be met either
by bond issues or tax increases.
It may be well to itemize here the new claims
demanded by the various departments. These, as re-
ported by the press are as follows:
*
Figure according to statement of Minister
of Railways representing the Finance Minister,
as reported in the press.

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
sum of Yen 200,000,000. Necessity of preventing
further demonstrations of agrarian unrest, as well
as promises of the Government, will prevent drastic
reduction of this claim. On the other hand, the
Yen 300,000,000 for relief work may be increased if
the Seiyukai pushes its demand to shorten the relief
program from three to two years.
The remaining claims of the other Ministries, even
if entirely disallowed, would affect the total demand
in only a minor degree.
It may be seen that in drafting the new budget
the Government is in a serious predicament. Not only
is the matter of committing the Government to such
a grandiose program extremely serious, but also the
method by which these funds are to be raised has created
a problem of the first magnitude.
During the two years of the Hamaguchi-Wakatsuki
administration, the late Finance Minister Inouye suc-
ceeded in reaucing the budget from Yen 1,736,000,000
to Yen 1,497,000,000 by dint of heroic economy. Mr.
Inouye was a firm believer in deflation, the gold
standard and a non-loan policy. At the end of that
Ministry(December, 1931) the national loans outstanding
stood at about Yen 6,000,000,000 which was fixed as
the extreme limit of safety in national indebtedness.*
Note: At the beginning of the 1932-33 fiscal year
beginning April,1932, the national bond issue stood
at Yen 6,187,650,000. New bonds issued or scheduled
to be issued during the current fiscal year amount
to Yen 840,770,000 and the amount to be redeemed
at Yen 34, 430,000. Thus, national bonds at the end
of March,1933 will aggregate Yen 6,993,990,000-
Figures from OSAKA MAINICHI).

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
Thus, within shortly more than a year since the
overthrow of Mr. Inouye's policies, the bond issue will
have increased by nearly a billion to approximately
Yen 7,000,000,000. Every indication points to further
loan increases of approximately a billion per year, to
meet new expenses which appear to become permanent charges.
In this connection Mr. Mitsuchi has stated" If this
(floating of a billion yen loan) is done, the national
debt will amount to Yen 10,000,000,000 in two or three
years, entailing interest payments at an annual rate
of Yen 500,000,000. In addition to this, we shall have
to continue our fixed payments for pensions and sinking
funds. These, together with the above-mentioned interest,
would call for Yen 1,250,000,000, absorbing practically
all of the Government's revenue".*
Mr. Mitsuchi was no doubt over-stating the case in
the interest of economy, inasmuch as a loan program of
this magnitude will result in an inflation of currency
* Note: The aggregate total interest payable
for Japan's na tional loan at the beginning of
the fiscal year 1932-33, commencing April,1933,
stood at Yen 315,000,000. It will be increased
by Yen 20,000,000, to Yen 335,000,000 at the
beginning of 1933-34. At the present rate,
which promises to be permanent, there will be
a yearly increase of Yen 1,000,000,000 indebted-
ness per year while the annual redemption, as
amended this year, is only Yen 34,430,000.
Figures from OSAKA MAINICHI.

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
economy. It advocates that at least the interest
on loans should be met by taxation, and feels uneasy
at the prospect of dependence on increasing loans.
According to Dr. Washio, extreme elements in the Army
go the length of advocating the conscription of wealth.
One would think that in consideration of the
stringency of the times, the logical solution to the
financial problem would be a general reduction in ex-
penditure. This, for political reasons, seems to be
impossible of accomplishment.
The Army and Navy, now
firmly in the saddle, demand funds for completion of
"defence programs" which were drawn up in the past but
postponed because of financial considerations during
less militaristic times. They now claim that in view of
the present world situation, the program must be completed.
No doubt it will be completed. It is safe to say that
whatever the sacrifice necessary, the money for the
military program will be found and appropriated.
Not the least remarkable feature of the present budget
situation is the brazen manner in which the
military have
pushed their way into the Government's financial councils.
General Araki was reported as stating on October llth"In
times of national emergency, such as the present, the
prompt execution of military plans is simply a matter of
course
...... No matter how the money is raised, the Army
budget admits of no curtailment".
1 The Osaka MAINICHI stated on October 13th that the
Army Chief of Finance Bureau called on the Chief of the
Account Bureau of the Finance Ministry and made strong
representations to that Ministry. He "intimated that
they

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
they(the Army) might produce their trump card at any
time, namely an emergency tax increase, if the budget
adjustment is disadvantageous to the Military". I am
not sure what is meant by this "trump card", but a
competent Japanese observer informs me that the Army
has in mind an emergency and possibly extra-legislative
tax imposition, something on the order of that imposed
during the Russo-Japanese war. This "emergency tax"
was eventually incorporated into the normal tax.
Comparatively little adverse comment on the new
budget demands has appeared in the press. There is
little to say, of course, other than that the demands
are preposterous in these hard times. This no editor
seems willing to say, probably because of the very real
danger of antagonizing the military, whose claims are
the largest. The public has enthusiastically supported
the military in all of its recent operations, and the
military has now taken advantage of its new found
popularity to push its demands for expansion. A re-
action may set in when the costs of military ctivity
are brought home to the public, but at present there
are few such indications.
That there does exist some feeling against the
military demands is evidenced by a rumor published by
the JIJI SHIMPO. This paper states," A movement has
been started by members of the Minseito and its sup-
porters to replace Premier Saito with General Ugaki,
the present Governor General of Chosen. It seems that
the principal objective of this movement is the curbing
of the influence of General Araki, Minister of War.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
1/
There is hereto appended an article in translation by
Dr. Seiki Hijikata, in which he discusses the financial
problem confronting the nation in balancing the budget.
This article has particular interest because the writer is
the President of the Bank of Japan. All informed parsons
10
in Japan know that military demands are the chief caise of
Japan's present budgetary difficulties, and it is therefora
interesting to note the attitude in regard to this problem
taken by various Japanese observers.
Dr. Hi jikata

=== Page 151 === (confidence 1.00)
2
Dr. Hi ikata carefully avoids criticising the military
claims.
On the contrary he states that at this juncture
any cutting into the milit ry expenditure cannot be dreamed
of, nor can there be any thought of retreating from the
position in Manchuria.
. His only suggestion to the military
is that they exercise economies in the maintenance of the
standing force so that something can be saved to apply toward
expenses in Manchuria.
The article as a whole is, however, an eloquent warning
of the real financial danger confronting the country in the
mounting drain on the treasury and in the inflation which is
sure to
follow an uncontrolleu loan policy.
His closing
sentence is most significant:
"All the sacrifices the
people are making today will have been to no purpose unless
adequate readjustment of the national finances is made in
good time and effectively".
It seems possible that Dr.
Hijikata values his own safety too much to allow him to
draw the obvious conclusion and oppose definitely the
increased military claims.
There is much evidence that there is an increasing
feeling of dismay in many quarters over the prospect of
ever-increasing milit ry expenditures coupled with the
national financial crisis.
Until recently there was hardly
a voice to be heard opposing any of the military propositions.
Now however, occasional statements and articles are to be
een which indicate that the public is becoming alarmed.
2,3,4/
There are hereto appended translations from three editorials
appearing in the Japanese newspapers which tend to confirm
this view.

National Life and Defence
Editorial (in part)
Nagoya SHIN AICHI,
October 30, 1932.
It was reassuring to hear Finance Minister Takahashi
say that finances are more significant than armaments, because
finances do not come from armaments and armaments are
impossible without finances.
Men really concerned about
the destiny of the country, he added, will not argue seriously
that finances can be disregarded where armaments are involved.
It is very pleasing to hear Mr. Takahashi's voice in so
wholesome an opinion at present, when a cert in inrluence is
so often alrectlng tne naulcn's iinances anu boin tne
seiyukai and minseito remain silent about the largest budget
ever known in Japan.
Finances that do not conform with national economy mean
ruin.
Any country that ignores a balance between revenue and
Because of
expenditure will be unable to obtain support.
their rnormous tusks and horns the mammoths perished in the
struggle for existence. The same fate may apply to nations
that have unwieldy armaments not in keeping with their
Nations do not depend solely on
national economic strength.
armaments.
Defence depends on combinations of economy,
Specialists
thought, education, customs and other factors.
are often likely to regard their own fields in an exaggerated
way.
They can see one tree from another in the forest, but
they do not know who to see the forest as a whole.

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.97)
Enoloaure No. 3
8
PromAAay
Tokyo.
The Budget of the Next Fiscal Year.
Editorial (in part)
TOKYO CHUGAI SHOGYO SHIMPO,
November 2, 1932.
Much has been said about the absolute necessity of
all classes of the Japanese people to unite as one body in
dealing with the Manchurian question.
In truth there is
no alternative for the Japanese but to do so in order to
tide over the present difficulty.
In view of the actual
conditions in Japan, however, it is doubtful whether the
people in all quarters are really so determined to take a
united action for the attainment of the object in view....
No one can tell how much the state expenditures will
be in the future.
In order to provide for the future, it
is deemed necessary to curtail expenditures as far as
possible at the present juncture.
Not even a single day
must be left idle in fostering the economic strength of
the people.
It is logical that where there is no finance
there is no defence.
At the present everything should not
be sacrificed for national defence.
From this viewpoint
we cannot regard as justified the remarkable increase of
military claiis
for the mere sake of national defences.
The same may be said of expenditures for various relief
measures.
Relief is most urgent, but in future there may
arrive a time when the popple may be called upon to sacrifice
themselves to the fullest extent. We warn the Government
that too much relief at the present moment may ultimately
drive the State to the point where the Government will find
it impossible to meet military needs......

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
2
the state's finances.
The Department of Finance has just cut down the
enormous new claims, and has approved appropriations
amounting to Yen 600,000,000 altogether.
Even these
appropriations are really a heavy drain upon the State's
finances.
Still we are obliged to accept the new
appropriations as a whole for the reason that the naval
and military authorities have ample reasons to lay their
new claims before the central treasury and that matters
concerning the army and navy must not be considered and
judged merely from the standpoint of the state finances.
We hope that the budget fer the next ieeal will be
compiled by adjusting properly the relations between the
present critical political situation and the financial
capacity of the state to bear these burdens.
What we
fear most at this juncture is that if carried through,
these enormous new claims may drive the State to the verge
of bankruptcy.
In the existing situation, the worsening
of the state finances may be unavoidable, but we hope that
the Government will compile the new budget so as to avoid
a collapse of the state finances.

(French Embassy)
(From -
REGARDING:
Conversation between the Secretary and the French
Ambassador in regard to Massenet's visit to Manchuria.
The Ambassador stated the Japanese Government was
trying to facilitate Massenet in his errand,only
on condition that he could get a loan from France
for them,that Massenet had some hopes,bu the Am-
bassador thought this was practically impossible.
fpg

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE UNDER SECRETARY
2
discussed Massenet's visit to say that Massenet
represented the French Government; he said he
hoped I would have this corrected immediately as
Massenet had no connection whatever wi th the French
Government; he said that his talk with Massenet had
very much discouraged him because Massenet had so
strongly pointed out the danger of a very serious
social crisis in Japan which might take a communistic
angle and might take a Fascisti angle, with socialism
intermixed. In any case, Massenet felt that the
immediate future of Japan was in a very perilous state;
he also made the report that Japanese troubles in
Manchuria could only grow worse for the reason that
certainly not more than one per cent of the people in
Manchuria had any use for Japan and that possibly even
sreil
that one per cent was opposed to Japanese domination.
The reason for this, according to Massenet, is that
the Chinese are ignored and made fun of by the Japanese
officials; Massenet spoke of one instance where he was
interviewing one of the principal Chinese leaders of
Manchukuo when the young Japanese officer with him
said "There is not much use talking to these people,
they

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE UNDER SECRETARY
3
they are all fools, in the hearing of the official.
The Ambassador pointed out that Japan, more than
any country in the world, was divided between men
of real intelligence, who now have no power, and the
army, which, although it has some excellent people at
the head, is dominated by under officers who are as
rotten a crowd as it is possible to imagine; he said
that, in any country underlings with a little power
are apt to be overbearing, but that this was abnormally
true in the case of Japan.
The Ambassador brought up the subject of the
Lytton Report, which he said he had just read in its
793.94
entirety; he feels that the recommendations in the
Report are untenable because they would never work;
he says that what he fears is that the League, dominated
by the small nations, will simply censure Japan for
all that it has done, without making any constructive
suggestions; that this will endanger the position of

In general, I agree with the logic of
Salisbury's criticism of the statements con-
tained in the attached despatch from Tokyo
under date November 18. At the same time,
however, it also seems apparent that a rapid
and in the main unexpectedly favorable
increase in Japan's export trade has occurred
since the depreciation of the yen which is
now equal to approximately twenty cents.
Last year Japan's adverse trade balance
amounted to 89,000,000 yen. This year, for
the first time,at least since l920, the trade
balance is reported to be favorable. That
the change has been rapid is evidenced by the
report of our own Department of Commerce
which
as recently as November 26 painted
a gloomy picture of Japanese trade with an
"unsatisfactory financial situation"
"political uncertainty" "decline in customs
receipts" "no increase in employment or
wages" "advance in cost of living" etc.,
whereas according to the last report from
the same souroe under date December 7 "steel
and industrial machinery industries are
operating at capacity and have advance orders
ranging from three to six months. The volume
of railroad freight traffic is increasing
and as
business has improved

I wish to refer to my despatch No. ll7 (undated
but written in early September), entitled "Present
Economic Conditions in Japan in Relation to Future
Contingencies", and especially to the summary thertof,
in which I stated as follows:
"The internal economic situation in Japan
has improved appreciably in the last two
months. In the early summer the position
of

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
are making large profits and have decided to decrease
the curtailment of output rate. The mills manufactur-
ing yarns and cloths for export are stated to be sold
out until the end of the year and the mills manufactu-
ring for domestic consumption, which were formerly bad-
ly affeoted by the depression, are again operating near-
ly on full time. The rayon plants are working at capacity
and are expanding their facilities. The steel mills, which
had been largely idle for the past two years, are booked
up with orders for some months to come and are expanding
their facilities. (This activity in the steel manufactu-
ring industry is partly due to military orders but is al-
so partly due to renewed commercial buying.) Premiums
are now being paid for quick delivery of steel. The cement
and woolen industries are also unusally active. The small-
er industries, such as those manufacturing toys, electrio
light bulbs, brushes, etc., are finding it difficult to fill
the orders coming in from abroad. Factories which had been
shut down for some time are reopening, industrial workers
are more easily finding employment, and wages are rising
slowly. The heavy industries are buying raw materials in
haste, partly because of the increasing demand for manu-
factured products and partly because of the anticipation
of higher prices. Mr. Mitsuchi, the Railway Minister,
states that the index number of industrial production in
Japan (based on 1913 as 100) is 239, as compared with 84
in

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
yen, but the volume ofexports has also inoreased
markedly. Imports are decreasing in volume, although
the yen value remains much the same as last year, be-
cause of the depreciation in value of the yen. Raw
cotton imports are fairly heavy and will increase toward
the end of the year, as Japanese mills arereported to
have contracted already for 600,000 bales of the new
American crop.
The visible excess of imports by the end of October
was down to Yen 55,758,000, or practically the same as
that of 1931 for the same period. If the present export
excess continues, as seems likely, the import excess for
1932 wi11 be down to Yen 25,000,000 or Yen 30,000,000.
However, heavy raw cotton imports toward the end of the
year may raise the import excess. At any rate, it seems
certain that the visible balance of trade will be less
unfavorable to Japan in 1932 than in any year since 1920.
A favorable balance of trade is predicted for l933.
Finanoe.
The money market is slack and the country banks have
been able partly to liquidate their frozen assets. The
feeling of panic, apparent among the large bankers of
Tokyo and Osaka in the spring and summer of this year, has
entirely disappeared. The yen is weak on foreign exchanges,
being now between $.20 and $.21, but this has not appre-
ciably disturbed the internal confidence of the country.
A1though

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
Although preparations were made for inflation of the
curreney, no inflation has taken place. The note is-
sue of the Benk of Japan at present is around Yen 984,000,000,
with a gold reserve of Yen 429,000,000. With the yen at
par, this is a coverage of 43 per cent; with the yen at
its present foreign exchange value of about $.2l, it is a
coverage of l02 per cent. Some six to seven hundred million
yen in government bonds are still to be issued this fiscal
year; if these are all taken up by the Bank of Japan, it is
estimated that the Bank will have to increase its note issue
by approximately 30 per cent of the total value of the bonds,
or say Yen 200,000,000. In this case, the coverage would
still be 85 per cent. Real inflation, therefore, is not
probable for the time, although if the huge bond issues pro-
posed for the next fiscal year are put through, real infla-
tion will be inevitable. Japanese financiers and economists
are not objecting to the bond issues for the present fiscal
year, with the small amount of inflation indicated, but they
are perturbed over the proposed issues for the 1933-34
fisoal year. They do not want inflation to a greater degree
than that which will becaused by the bond issues of the
present fiscal year.
The international balance of payments for this year
promises to be much more favorable to Japan than was antici-
pated earlier in the year. The visible unfavorable balance
of trade has decreased remarkably during the past few months,
whi1o

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.98)
一7-
while shipping receipts, which are invisible exports,
have increased considerably when figured in yen, owing
to the fact that most shipping receipts are based on
dollar or sterling rates. The interest and amortization
charges on Japanese loans issued abroad have of course
been greatly increased, in yen figures,by the fall in the
yen. The Japanese Government and Japanese corporations
must pay abroad some fifty to sixty million dollars an-
nually, as service on loans. At the present rate of ex-
change, this means finding some 250 to 300 million yen for
payment abroad. It has been found in recent months (in
connection with the operation of the Law for the Prevention
of the Flight of Capital) that around half of the bonds
issued by the Japanese Government and Japanese corporations
have been purchased by Japanese banks, trust companies and
individuals, who wished to put their money into some more
stable currency than yen. About half of the money paid
abroad for the servicing of loans, therefore, will flow
back to Japan, making the net payments abroad for this pur-
pose not more than 150 million yen. The external payments
for this year are therefore not nearly as large as was
thought six months ago.
The yen is weak on foreign exchanges, but this is large-
ly due to the fest that importers have been actively making
their import settlements for as much as six months ahead,
while exporters have been holding their export bills off the
market, hoping for a further drop in the yen. In view of
the fairly favorable balance of international payments and
tho

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
the high coverage for the note issue, it does not seem
reasonable for the yen to fall further, and some observers
expect it to rise in the near future, although a rise be-
yond $.25 is not anticipated. A discussion is now taking
place regarding the point at which the yen should be sta-
bilized, some believing that $.20 is the best value, while
others believe that $.l5 will be better, as this point
will further decrease the debts of the Government and of
the debtor class. It will be noted that, with the present
fall in the value of the yen, the debts of the nation (ex-
in
cept those/foreign currencies) have been decreased, when
caloulated in gold, by 58 per cent.
The budget is the biggest adverse factor in the finan-
cial situation. The proposed ordinary budget for next year
does not balance by some 300 million yen, which sum must
be raised by means of loans. In addition, large extraordi-
nary expenditures are proposed, which will raise, if approved,
the borrowings to around a billion yen. Against this adverse
factor, however, can be placed the facts that the national
debt of Japan is not large, in its ratio to the national
wealth of the country and that the taxation revenue will
naturally increase with the expansion and easier circulation
of money, rendering it possible to carry the additional bur-
den of debt. It will not, in all probability, be safe to
increase the burden of debt beyond that proposed for the
coming fiscal year, but it is believed that Japanese econo-
mists

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.98)
.- 10 -
will rise to greater heights than ever before in Japan.
Gradually, however, the world will impose tariffs against
the advantage which Japanese goods possess in the depreciated
yen, while prices will rise in Japan and with them costs of
production will become greater. In addition there will be
increased taxation burdens because of the growing national
debt and the cost of military operations, as well as the
cost of the paternalistic policy which the Japanese Govern-
mendt adopts toward agriculture, industry and finance.
These adverse factors will gradually make thenselves felt
and will eventually cause a collapse of the artificial pros-
perity brought about by the depreciation of the currency.
When the collapse comes, there will be a depression worse
than the one just past, because in the meantime industry
will have over-expanded and production will be greatly in
excess of the normal needs of the country.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

I suggest that Dr. Wallace has missed
the signifioance of the despatch. On pages
3 and 4 the Embassy points out that for a
considerable period before the Manchuria
affair, "the army was not popular with the
publio" and that the people were wearied of
military things and costs. The Embassy
further states that "the popularity which
the military have enjoyed during the past
year is the result of a war psyohology".
I am not oertain whether Dr. Wallace
believes that it would be possible to apply
economic pressure and yet avoid (a) accentua-
ting the Japanese war psyohology or (b) war.
My considered and definite opinion is that
it would be possible to avoid neither, and
that the Japanese "people would question the
advisability of military leadership" only
when "war psyohology" is no longer a factor.

There is hereto appended & translation of an addrs
delivered by Baron S. Goh, President of the Tokyo Chamber
of Commerce and Industry, at a dinner on November 24 which
was attended by a large number of high officials, including
General Araki, Minister of War. This speech has received
considerable publicity because of the prominence of the
speaker and guests.
Baron Goh's speech is mainly concerned with state
finances

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
finances and the danger of large expenditure in depressed
times. However, the chief interest in the speech lies in
the fact that the author places responsibility for the
nation's grave financial situation directly on the military.
His remarks about the unproductive nature of military
expenditure were apparently stronger than the appended
translation would indicate(the draft was received directly
from Baron Goh at the request of the Embassy and it may
well have been edited), as the press reports that General
Araki responded in an angry and sarcastic manner. From
study of the speech I have reason to believe that Baron
Goh's intention was to point out to the military the
necessity for reduction in their expenditures.
It is significant that since this speech was delivered
there are increasing indications of opposition, if not
hostility on the part of the public to the military.*
This feeling of opposition seems to have appeared as the
result of popular concern over the unprecedeiely large
budget recently drawn up, in which military costs are
plainly demonstrated.**
The Embassy has repeatedly ex-
pressed the opinion that the stone over which the military
may fall is money. Of course there have long existed elements
in the country which opposed the military program of the
past year, but which have remained largely hidden because of
popular sympathy with the military cause. These elements,
of which Baron Goh is representative, are now becoming ap-
parent again. With popular sympathy for the military ebbing,
these
* See despatch No. 165 of Uctober 21, 1932.
** See despatch No. 221 of December 2, 1932

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
Siberian and Shantung adventures wearied the people of
military things and costs.
After the Washington Conference military prestige
steadily declined . In 1922 and again in 1925, in response
to popular demand, considerable cuts were made in Army
personnel and organization. The London Treaty was ratified
in the face of fierce but unavailing military opposition.
Had the Shidehara diplomacy been a little firmer in handling
Chinese affairs, the military might yet be well under the
control of the civil authorities.
The popularity which the military have enjoyed during
the past year is the result of a war psychology which is
not limited to Japanese. This popularity is not, therefore,
a constant quantity. There is every possibility that the
pendulum may swing in the other direction, and that the
Army may again be put on the defensive against a popular
demand for retrenchment. When the costs of the recent
military activities are brought home to the pubilc in
the shape of higher taxes, increased costs of living due to
inflation, and heavy national indebtedness, it is logical
to predict a measure of disillusionment and resentment
against those responsible.
As I have pointed out above, the thinking people
of the nation have long recognized the danger, and feared
the consequence, of the overseas operations. There are
2,3/
hereto appended translations of two editorial articles from
influential Japanese papers which would indicate that the
public , as represented by the press, is turning away
from glorification of the military. Certainly nothing as
frank as these articles has appeared in the press regarding
the

SPEECH BY BARON GOH ON NOVEMBER 24, 1932.
The financial administration of our country has
undergone a remarkable and abnormal change in recent years.
Since the year 1929, the balance between revenues and ex-
penditures of the State has been maintained only by the
utilization of surpluss, or the transfer of money accruing
from the issue of loans for rehabilitation following the
great earthquake, or the abolition of the amortization fund.
Under the circumstances, revenues of every year showed a
deficit.
This deplorable state of affairs became especially
remarkable with the occurrence of the Manchorian incident
and the adoption of relief measures for the sake of farmers,
laborers and other classes in distress.
Thus the balance
of the budget was totally upset. In the current 1932-33
fiscal year, ordinary and extraordinary revenues are put at
Yen 1,320,000,000, while expenditures amount to Yen 1,940,000,000,
creating a deficit of Yen 620,000,000. In the next 1933-34
fiscal year, revenues are estimated at Yen 1,340,000,000 and
expenditures at over Yen 2,230,000,000, resulting in a de-
ficit of about Yen 900,000,000. Such enormous expenditure
as this is unavoidable in view of the present extraordinary
situation at home and abroad, but under the Government's
policy of meeting the deficit by loans, the flotation of new
domestic loans for the current year will amount to Yen
780,000,000, while the issue of 1oans for the next 1933-34
fiscal year wil1 reach more than Yen 1,100,000,000. During
a

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
about a revival in financial circles. I am confident
that this revival will. grow in dimensions when the above-
mentioned measures shall have been carried out thoroughly.
However, just pause for a moment and think quietly, and we
wil1 see that there is a question as to how far domestic
industries will be affected in future by the use of enormous
funds for relief measures. A large portion of these
enormous funds is apportioned for the army and the navy. I
am well aware that the expenditures required for Manchurian
affairs and for national defenses are essential for the
existence of the State. However, viewed from a purely econo-
mic and industrial standpoint, the expenditures above-mentioned
are to be spent on unproductive enterprises. Moreover, the
expenditures for public works in various prefectures are
necessary in order to save the people. from distress. It is,
however, feared that a great portion of public work may be
of a nature not so urgent and thus not of assistance to
economic rehabilitation.
As above stated, the deficit in
revenues will be met by
the issue of loans. New enterprises started on the basis of
loans are nothing but unproductive undertakings from an economic
standpoint. It is hardly necessary to point out that the pay-
ment of principal and interest on these loans wil prove a
heavy burden of the people in the future. From the viewpoint
of the State and financial administration, we are apparently
facing an extraordinary situation. As the Government authori-
ties think, the financial administration of our country is
decidely in an unhealthy and unwholesome condition. But the
expenditures required by the army and the navy as well as for
relief measures are necessary and must be made.
The revenues
are

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
retrenchment in financial administration as well as in
economic circles. I then expressed the hope.that the
Government would not confuse financial administration
with economics. To this, the late Mr. Inouye made a res-
ponse. It is an undeniable fact that under a retrenchment
policy of finances a retrenchment of finances of the State,
which is the greatest consumer, will affect economic circles
to a certain extent. In future when measures are taken for
the adjustment of finances, I trust that the appropriate
authorities wil' help and protect productive enterprises
as far as possible.

without thorough harmony between the people and mili-
tary men.
In a section of the public, opposition is privately
manifested against the military authorities, although
this opposition is not made public because the military
authorities are now overshadowing all other factions. We
entertain grave anxiety lest such a situation may ulti-
mately bring about a disunion between the people and
the military authorities and thereby endanger the national
defenses for the future. Believing that a frank exchange
of views adds to proper understanding, we deem it our
duty to call the military men's attention to the above
point at this opportunity of commenting on the alleged
plan of the military men to suggest a financial policy to
the Government.

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
attack each other. It is not, therefore, impossible
that they will direct their criticism against the
military authorities.
The question is whether the military authorities
deserve criticism. Since the issue of the Manchurian
affair, the activities of the military authorities have
been spectacular. It is even said that they have gone a
little further than necessary in some cases. Whether
such blunders were actually committed by them is not
clearly known. This question should be examined and
established on facts and realities. We are of the opinion
that such excesses, if any, should be overlooked in view
of the exigency of the stuation under which they had to
operate. It would not be of any avail to talk of the
past. If anything is to be wished, such wish ought to
be confined to the future. We trust that the military
authorities will be magnanimous enough to listen to any
criticism from a section of the public as regards alleged
excessive actions on their part.
For several years before the outbreak of the
Manchurian incident, the attitude of the people toward
the military authorities was not quite reassuring to the
latter. In some section of the people, even the attitude
that the military was little better than a useless
organization was manifested. The Manchurian incident
was naturally an opportunity for the military authorities
to give an object lesson to the people and a practical
answer to this indifferent attitude of the people toward
them. Viewed in this light, the operations of the military
authorities

227
Notes taken after attending a meeting of the
National League of Japanese University Professors.
Approximately 300 people were present, including
Count Uchida, General Araki, Admiral Okada(Navy Minister),
andNagai (Overseas Minister). Diplomats present were
Troyanovsky (Soviet), Hultman(Swede), Dr. Kolb(German),
Cunningham(British), and Bao (Manchukuo). Speakers
were Uchida, Araki, Okada, Nagai and Dr. Inoue, Professor
Emeritus of Tokyo Imperial University. All speeches either
very formal or perfunctory except those of Nagai and Inoue,
which I will touch later. The military were much in evidence;
at least ten general officers present.
This was the first meeting of a new organization.
The question in my mind was: what is this all about? What
is the reason for this meeting and why are the diplomats
invited? Dr. Kolb said: "This is all to demonstrate
that the thinkers of Japan are united in support of the
present Japanese program". I believe he was right.
Evidence: much talk of similar organizations abroad and
their influence in national affairs; all speakers referred
to present critical situation of Japan in international
affairs, Count Uchida going so far as to speak of "pressure
from abroad"; repeated reference to Japan and Manchukuo and
the justice of Japan's actions; the presence of a large military
contingent.
Nagai rather left the prepared track, it seemed to me.
He said Japan's greatness and power, and Japan's influence in
the world as one of the five great powers, is not due to her
military

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
military triumphs alone. Her greatness is due to her
thinkers, to her scientific and cultural achievements and
leadership in the world of thought.
To me this sounded
like heresy coming from a Japanese.
Old Dr. Inoue was more unorthodox. He reviewed Japanese
history in terms of Bun and Bu(literary and military). He
said the two must work together to create a true culture.
History shows that whenever one is neglected the nation
suffers. During the Heian period Japan became dilettante
and effiminate because of too uch emphasis on culture.
During the Kamakura period Japan became rough and un-
cultured because of the predominance of military idead.
I wondered if this speech did not contain an implied
rebuke to the military. It happened that I was placed next
to Dr. Inouye at dinner following the speeches, and I took
the occasion to ask him what he meant. He was most frank
in reply. He said"Yes, I feel that the cultural and
1
literary are being neglected in favor of the military,and
wished to point out that military prowess alone is useless".
I received the very definite impression from the meeting
that whoever organized it was attempting to get the scholars
and professors of the country lined up with the military,
and incidentally to show foreign representatives that the
national mind was unified in regard to Japan's overseas
operations. At the same time I got just as strong an im-
pression that the scholars were inclined to kick over the
traces and to demonstrate that they were not by any means
reconciled to the present dominating military influence.
W.T.Turner.
