regarded seriously and that conditions in New York City
are the governing factor. So long as Mitsui branch New
York reports conditions sound there no transfers will be
FILED
made.

Shanghai adventures by all Departments of the Government
are designated in official accounts as "expenses for the

tures by various Departments of the Government for the months
of February and March 1933 not authorized by previous supple-
mentary budgets. The necessary money will be obtained by
loans.

Thus far none of these loans has been raised in the
open money market, but have been absorbed by the Bank of
Japan and the Deposit Bureau of the Department of Finance,
togehher with other revenue loans raised to balance the budget.
A law* was promulgated on February 7, 1933, which permits
the Government to float loans for defraying expenditures on
account of the Manchurian affair to a total of Yen 491,000,000,
amending an earlier law which permitted the floating of a
1oan of Yen 260,000,000.
Thus by March 1934, in two and one half years of activity
in Manchuria and China, it is estimated that the nation will
have spent nearly Yen 600,000,000. With this may be compared
the cost of the four year Japanese campaign in Siberia,
which amounted to Yen 700,000,000**. It is not strange

Department of State
Despatch No. 304.
Date: February 24, 1933.
Subject: Cost of Japanese Military Activities in
Manchuria and China.
Summary:
The cost of Japan's overseas military acti-
vities from September 1931 to March 1934, is ap-
proximately Yen 563,450,000.
Expenditures in connection with Manchuria will
amount to approximately 15 per cent of the nation's
ordinary revenue during the coming fiscal year.
So far, loans amounting to 323,000,000 yen
have been raised to meet the expenses of the
Manchuria and Shanghai affairs. They have all
been absorbed by the Bank of Japan and the Deposit
Bureau of the Finance Department.
The Manchuria affair apparently will continue
indefinitely as a financial liability from which

FOREWORD
This report emphasizes the financial relation-
ship between the Amerioan and Japanese markets.
Other faotors bearing upon the market such as the
Manchurian situation, the world-wide economic de-
pression and military expenditures have been con-
sidered but have not been stressed. Day to day
fluotuations in the market such as those caused by
month-end settlements and sudden demands for funds
by large interests have been ignored.
The purpose of the report is to give a picture
of the close relationship of money rates in Japan
and the United States and an estimate of possible
future developments in Japan as affected by Ameri-
can policies.

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.98)
SECTION I
Review of the Japanese Money Market during
the Year 1932
In the first half of 1932, money in Japan was
fairly tight due to various unfavorable factors prin-
oipally among whioh was the deorease of loanable funds
following the outflow of gold whioh in December of
193l had foroed Japan to reimpose the gold embargo.
Tightening of the market was accentuated by the situ-
ation at Shanghai in February, followed by a minor
banking orisis in Nagoya in March. During the months
of April and May the market became easier due to re-
payments on treasury bills and a slackening in demand
for business funds, largely caused by the depressed
state of Japanese industry.
In July the enaotment of the law to prevent the
flight of oapital further eased the money market.
During August the Bank of Japan lo wered its disoount
rate to l.2 sen per diem per hundred yen while private
banks lowered interest on deposits to 4.2%. As a se-
quonoe interest on postal savings bank deposits was
lowered from 42% to 3%. This resulted in a considerable
flow of funds from the Government institution into
private banks further easing the market.
During the latter part of the year interest rates
on overnight loans and commeroial paper continued to
fall. In December 1932 and January of 1933, the open
market selling of treasury notes and short term rice
bills by the Bank of Japan absorbed surplus funds of
private banks, thus checking an inflationary tendenoy
tewaras rising priees in the stook markets by with-
sonlasive funds.
Distribution

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.98)
-5 -

During
1932, 448,836,000 yen worth of debent ure issues were
subsoribed while new capital stook payments amounted
to 149,634,000 or a total distribution of new capital
of ¥598,470,000. 0f this amount ¥215,504,000 was ab-
sorbed by banking enterprises, 90,336,000 by manufao-
turing industries, 102,796,000 by electric and gas
utilities, 126,229,000 by traffic enterprises,
1,500,000 by mining enterprises and 62,102,000 by al1
others.

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
SECTION II
The Japanese Money Market and its Relation to
the American Market
The reoent Amerioan banking orisis followed by
the Presidential order olosing Amerioan banks and
imposing a gold embargo has demonstrated the inter-
relationship between the Amerioan and the Japanese
money markets. Important Japanese financiers have
stated that future develapments in the Japanese
money market will largely depend upon policies to
be followed in the United States. With this in
view a study of the Japanese money market immedi-
ately before, during and after the American bank-
ing orisis is of interest.
As previously mentioned the latter half of l932
was marked by considerable ease of the money market
indioated by falling interest rates and increase in
bank deposits as against loans. Interest rates were
temporarily increased due to year-end settlements in
the latter part of December 1932 but soon weakened,
although not to the previous low levels. On March
second, immediately before the development of the
American banking orisis, interest rates on over-
night, unconditional, and ordinary loans were 8.5
rin and up ward per 100 yen per diem (10 rin equals
1 sen); on spinning bills 10.5 to 12 rin, on com-
meroial bills 1l to 17 rin and on second commercial
bills 14 to 18 rin. The Bank of Japan was active in
the open market, selling Government and short term
securities in an endeavor to check the use of sur-
plus funds in the possession of private banks from
finding

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
finding their way into speculative activity. It was
estimated that at this date, Maroh 2nd, approximate-
ly $150,000,000 was held for American exohange by
Japanese banks. At the time of the imposition of the
American gold embargo and the freezing of all credits
by the olosing of banks, this sum had been reduced
to approximately 100,000,000. When the gold em-
bargo was announced all foreign exohange markets in
Japan closed as did all other stock and commodity
exohanges exoept the Osaka rice exchange. Immediate
reaotion to the Amerioan orisis was fear upon the
part of Japanese traders that Japan's silk ind us try
would still further be depressed and that present
holdings by the textile industries of large stocks
of raw cotton would be reduced in value due to a
rise in the yen. Large industrial and public utili-
ty companies in Japan owing #801,531,000 of foreign
debentures, mostly in dollars, were of the opinion
that little relief would be afforded by a rise in
value of the yen inasmuoh as the debenture hold-
ings were expressed in gold dollars of a presoribed
weight and fineness of gold. Due to these fears
the money market sharply eased.
Interest rates on over-night loans fell from
8.5 rin per 100 yen per diem on Maroh 2nd to 6 rin
during the height of the orisis, while interest
rates on commeroial and ordinary bills followed
suit. Deposits in all Japanese banks had increased
during February to 5,986,000,000, the largest
sums on deposit sinee June l93l, while in the same
period loans by banks had decreased to ¥5,040,000,000,
the lomosee
 ofoutstanding loans since the

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
-8 -
the faot that public utilities, business corporations,
manufacturing industries, and other commercial en-
terprises were not in med of large funding opera-
tions and to the loss of seourity values suffered
by the stock market reaction from the boom period
of December 1932.
Large Government spending on relief and for
military purposes had resulted in inoreased bank
deposits which were likewise added to by with-
drawals in the Government postal savings bureau.
To this situation in which the Bank of Japan ex-
perienced further great difficulty in holding in-
terest rates at January levels was now added the
uncertainty as to the Amerioan banking si tuation.
It was confidently believed by financiers and tra-
ders that America was about to embark on a policy of
inflation which would necessitate a rise in the yen
exohange rate thus damaging the Japanese silk and
textile exporting trade. During the period of the
finanoial orisis in America, banks therefore refused
to make loans to commercial enterprises and instead
sought to purohase Government securities and short
term bills from the Bank of Japan.
Situation since the American banking crisis. With
the reopening of American banks, the money market
tended to relax somewhat due to inoreasing confidence
in the situation. The Yokohama Specie Bank had em-
barked upon a polioy of sterling purohases in an en-
deavor

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.98)
- 9 -
deavor to prevent the rise of the yen as against the
pound sterling. To finance these purchases, short
term oredits had been bought up in the local markets,
which tightened the market in this field.
The rise was however but temporary and was due in
large measure to reaotion from the panicky earlier
period. As the disparity between bank deposits and
loans continues to grow, banks are finding it neces-
sary either to reduce interest rates upon deposits
or to employ surplus funds either in buying Govern-
ment seourities or in making commercial loans. This
is evidenoed by the fact that during the last two
months 140,000,000 of Government securities have been
purohased. Interest rate on call money therefore
dropped from 7.5 rin on Maroh 14th to 7 rin at the
date of writing as the banks endeavored to force
their surplus holdings into the market.
Future tendencies of the money market. Future
tendencies in the money aret will of course basi-
oally be determined by the condition of Japanese in-
dustry. Little change in funding operations of Japa-
nese commercial enterprises is anticipated and there-
fore conditions in the money market will largely be
determined by extraneous factors. Inflation has as
yet brought forth no demands for increased construo-
tion work inasmuch as the majority of Japanese in-
dustries are still operating at a peroentage of
capaoity. Until larger demands for commeroial fund-
ing develop, money rates will inevitably remain easy
unless the efforts of the Bank of Japan to prevent
speculetive astivity are without effeot.
Somo

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
Some traders believe that the open market ao-
tivities of the Bank of Japan will cease inasmuch as
tho Bank's holdings of treasury no tes and short term
bills have been materially deoreased by its selling
operations of the last two months. Unless these
treasury notes and short term bills continue to be
offered, it is believed that the Bank of Japan's dis-
count rates will be lowered and that private banks
will have to cut interest rates on fixed deposits. It
is estimated that considerable funds now on fixed de-
posit would, under those circumstances, be used for
buying seourities and that prices would therefore rise.
Some tendency on the part of banks toward investment
in seourities in the open market and underwriting of
loans has been noticed during the past few days.
The above estimate of the future of the money market
is based upon the assumption that America will not in-
flate its currency. In the event that currency is
inflated the yen will rise, thus seriously affeoting
Japan's export trade which is largely responsible for
the amall boom in indus try. If this should happen
the money market will be presented with the anomaly
of an easy market as evidenced by falling interest
rates but with the banks at the same time afraid to
underwrite loans for oommercial enterprises due to
uncertainty as to Japan's future in the export trade.
The Bank of Japan has announoed that efforts
will be made to prevent the yen rising above 22, al-
though it is problematical if enough funds are at
hand, to establish an exohange stabilization fund
able

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.98)
-11 -
able to stand the pressure in the event that American
ourrency is inflated. In its essentials future money
rates in Japan will be largely dependent upon the
policies adopted in America. External affairs such as
Japan's withdrawal from the League and the possibili-
ties of economic sanctions will likewise affect the
market although the probability of such an issue has
already been discounted. Generully speaking it is
anticipated that ease in the market will cont inue al-
though operations by the Bank of Japan will prevent
any sudden change in the tone of the market.

=== Page 276 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
Together with the general account supplementary
budget reported in the Embassy's despatch No. 304 of
February 24, 1933, these financial bills represent
the total appropriation made during the recent Diet
session for expenditures in the fiscal year 1932-33.
Financial legislation for that year is now considered
as completed.
Inasmuch as appropriations for the 1932-33 fiscal
year were made in haphazard manner in several Diet
sessions and on the basis of a working budget carried
over from the previous year, calculation of the total
expenditure for the 1932-33 fiscal year is extremely
involved. An attempt will be made by the Embassy to
prepare such calculation in a future despatch.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

REGARDING:
Japanese attitude with regard to Money and Credit and other
Economic Matters to be discussed at the Preliminary conversations tothe
Monetary and Economic Conference, London.
411
MOT

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
-2- No. 101 from Tokyo.
to offer but are willing to consider any proposal which
may be made in the light of the best interests of Japan.
Their general attitude may be sumned up in the words of
Fukaf, one of the delegates, who is revorted in the press
to have said just before leavins for Washington: (BEGIN
GRAY) "There are two aspects to international cooperation.
There is unconditional cooperation to promote the common
interests of the world. There is cooperation to further
the interests of any one nation. I hold to the latter
viewpoint. Ir ordor to promote Japan's interests, Japan
will cooperate with other nations on certain points." (END
GRAY)
One. POLITICAL MATTERS.
(a) Consultative Pact. Because of their experience
with the League the Japanese view with alarm any attempt
to implement the Kellogg Pact. They fear that a
consultative pact which provides for thc definition of an
aggressor will react on Japan. It is claimed that the
same circumstances which caused the Japanese
break with
the League will cause them to accept a consultative pact
only with reservations excluding Far Eastern affairs from
its scope.
(b) Asiatic Monroe Doctrine.
Ishiihimsolf advocates an attempt to obtain recognition
790.94
of Japan as the "guardian of the peace of Asia" rather
than an attompt to renew the Lansing-Ishii agreement or to
establish

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
-3- No. 101 from Tokyo.
establish an Asiatic Monroe Doctrine. It is not knovm
whether or not the Governmont favors Ishii's views but
obsorvors beliove that some attempt will be made to
establish the doctrine that western powers should not
interfere in Asiatic affairs.
(c) Disarmament.
As oxplainod in my despatch No.365 the Japanese have
no wish to disarm under present conditions. They are
inclined to view disarmament as a political problem and
probably will insist upon a review of Far Eastern political
questions in connection therewith. They may intend to
consider disarmament in con cction with paragraph (b)
above.
Two. ECONOMIC MATTERS.
(a) Money and Credit.
The return to the gold standard and the stabilization
of currency is favored in principle but it is claimed that
it is difficult for Japan to accomplish these ends because
of the small gold reserve and the weakness of Japanese
currency. A return to the gold standard would therefore
require redistribution of gold reserves among the nations
and stabilization of exchange would require assistance
from the United States in the form of credits or exchange
operations. The Japanese wish to avoid bringing the yen
back to its former level in terms of the gold dollar.
Apparently they would prefer to stabilize it at somewhere
around twenty cents but might compromise at twenty five
cents

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
-5- No. 101 from Tokyo
domestic rice prices and are unwilling to abolish these
restrictions.
(e) Tariffs.
A reasonable lowering of tariff barriers is approved
in principle but a general flat reduction of tariffs
is opposed on the ground that the Japanese tariff level
is already comparatively low. Bilateral or multilateral
reciprocal tariff conventions are favored.
(f) Organization of Production and Trade.
The desirability of readjusting intornational
real sed but it is
production and consumption of commoditics is/not (repeat
not) considered feasible to acco lish this by inter-
national convention because of puculiar conditions in each
country.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 1, 1933.
##
The report of May 9, 1933, of the
Military Attaché at Tokyo, states that,
according to the Statistical Bureau of
the Cabinet, the national wealth of Japan
at the end of 1930 was Yen 110,000,000,000.
Twelve per cent is Government owned, 4%
public owned and 84 per cent private
owned. The national wealth is Yen 1700
per capita.
The national wealth has increased
by Yen 7,800,000,000 since 1924. However,
if the method of calculation used in 1924
were used for the l930 figures, it would
be shown that the national weaith in 1930
was Yen 122,000,000,000 less than it was
in 1924.

Discussion of Budget for 1934-35.

The Secretary of State,

Washington.
Sir:
The season for discussion of the 1934-35 budget was
opened when, at a recent Cabinet Council meeting, the
Finance Minister stated that the Tax Readjustment Committee
has set to work on the study of proposed tax increases. Mr.
Takahashi, who stoutly opposed tax increases last year when
the largest budget in Japanese history was drawn up, is now
apparently reconciled to the inevitable.
Last fall when this year's budget was framed, grave
apprehension

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
apprehension was expressed on all sides at the unprecedented
size of the expenditure in the face of a dwindling revenue.
The feeling was general that a repetition of this budget
would force the country into serious financial straits. It
appears now that a repetition is at hand. I seems certain
that expenditure will not be recuced below Yen :,s00,o00,000*
and that revenue wi11 not exceed Yen 1,500,000,000, - in
round figures the sums for the current fiscal year. On the
contrary, it seems that expenditure will increase on account
of increased demands from the military. The War Office is
reported to be demanding a total of Yen 518,000,000, an in-
crease of about 80 million yen over the current year's ap-
propriation. The Navy, coming out bolcly for the "second
replacement plan" held in abeyance since the London Naval
Conference, is demanding abut Yen 430,000,000, or an increase
of approximately 60 million over the current appropriation.
Mr. Takahashi has expressed the hope that in view of
recent appreci: tion in the price of farm products and other
improved circumstances in rural districts since the relief
appropriations were made last year, savings can be made in
this appropriation. He also seems to think that expenditures
for the "Manchurian affair" which amount to 186 million this
year, can be reduced as a result of the close of the Jehol
campaign and the armistice in north China. Another expenditure
in

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
sulting from the decline of the yen, to make tax collection
easier. Some increase in revenues is certain, but no accurate
estimate can be made until the end of the current fiscal year.
The burden of taxes in Japan falls heaviest on the classes
with small income, because of the multiplicity of consumption
taxes and because of land taxes affecting the small farmer.
The Army insisted, last year, that taxes affecting the classes
with large incomes be raised, such as inheritance, progressive
income, an capital taxes. The Army capitulated to Mr. Takahashi's
resistance, but may be expected to take a stronger stand this
year, particularly if it's budgetary demands are not satisfied.
Another factor in the matter of tax increases is the at-
titude of thepolitical parties. Both the Seiyukai and Minseito
are studying plans for tax increases. The Seiyukai is suspected
of presenting some plan which will embarrass the Government in
an effort to overthrow the present Cabinet. It desires the
authority - and the spoils - which by reason of its parliamen-
tary majority it feels entitled to. The MIYAKO SHIMBUN states
editorially: "Unless all the signs are at fault, the completion
of the new budget will be the signal for the Seiyukai to re-open
the question of withdrawal of its Ministers from the present
(combination) cabinet". Mr. Hatoyama, the Education Minister,
1s reported to have expressed the belief that the Cabinet will
be threatened by another crisis about September, when the Go-
vernment will have completed the task of compiling the budget
estimates. Either party probably would gladly use the question
of taxation as a stick with which to embarrass the Government.
Mr. Takahashi's position is unenviable. He has to balance

Tokyo's despatch No. 603 of Decem-
ber 9, 1933, is a very well prepared re-
port on the draft budget for the fiscal
year 1934-1935. I suggest that you read
it in full.
It points out that the original
estimates of the various departments of
the Japanese Government amountato more
than Yen 2,800,000,000. The Fihance
Minister made a strong effort to limit
the tota1 to Yen 2,050,000,000, but due
to the pressure from military and naval
quarters, a compromise was finally reached
at Yen 2,111,000,000. The despatch states
that while this would be some 200,000,000
yen less than the budget for the present
fiscal year, it is in fact somewhat larger
and will be the largest budget ever adopted
by the Japanese Government. The difference
is explained by the fact that certain of
the enterprises of the Department of Com-
munications have been removed from unaer
t he general budget and are to be governed
by independent accounts.

Mr. Takahashi, the Finance Minister, set himself reso-
lutely to effect all possible economies in the preparation
of this budget and fixed as his limit the figure of Yen
2,050,000,000.
The original demands of the several Minis-
tries amounted to more than Yen 2,800,000,000.
Pressure
from the army and navy finally forced the Finance Ministry
to concede some of the demand, bringing the budget up to
the final figure -. Yen 2,111,537,000..
This budget appears to be appreciably smaller than
that of last year (2,239,525,000 - excluding supplementary
budgets) but another factor changes the situation.
The
business
*Figures from Tokyo Nichi Nichi.

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
Allotments to the remaining departments vary little from
those of the previous year.
The cuts in the allotments to the Home Department
and Agriculture and Forestry Department are at the expense
of relief measures for the distressed rural districts.
Refusal to grant funds for farm relief is apt to bring on
further difficulties for Mr. Takahashi.
Much dissatisfac-
tion is evident, even in the army, which is drawn largely
from rural districts. The allotment to the Finance Department
was cut in view of the improved exchange rate versus the
dollar and consequent decreased payment on foreign loan
interests. As explained above, the decreased expenditure
The
of the Communications Department is apparent only.
real increases were made in the army and navy allotments,
which will be discussed later.
The enormous new budget will be financed much as that
of the present year. Or inary revenue, or that accruing from
taxes and state enterprises, is estimated at Yen 1,248,000,000.
Taxes are said to have increased by about Yen 100,000,000
over last year, due to improved business con itions, but I
am unable as yet, in the absence of official figures on
budget income, to explain why in the figures available revenue
seems to have decreased rather than increased. The deficit
is to be met largely by "red ink" bond issues amounting to
Yen 785,335,000, which sum must be added to the national debt.
The latter, officially estimated to amount to Yen 7,809,100,000
at the end of November 1933, will thus be increased to approxi-
mately Yen 8,600,000,000 during the fiscal year 1934-35, with
probable

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
sums poured into the manufacture of munitions uring the
past two years have given a strong fillip to Japanese in-
dustry and created an appearance of prosperity unique
throughout the world. But this has been done on borrowed
money:
loans raised this far through the Bank of Japan
and the Deposit Bureau, amounting each year to about
1,000,000,000. The national debt has more than doubled
since 1922 and will stand at nearly 8 billion yen by the end
of December 1933. Obviously this situation cennot continue.
Either a program of retrenchment must follow, or else Japan-
ese currency will follow the mark and rouble in a financial
crash. Dr. Washio writes:
"The fight on the budget shows that Japan has
perforce already crossed the irretrievable
cross-road of inflation but is still at a
distance of looking back over her shoulder."
In the compilation of the present year's budget Minis-
ter Takahashi spoke optimistically of the country's ability
to bear a debt of even 10 billion yen, and refused to con-
sider raising taxes on the score that the revival of indus-
try must not be hindered by new imposts. He still refuses
to impost new taxes, but has apparently lost much of his
optimism. Although he has said but little recently his
stubborn efforts to restrict expenditure is evidence enough
of his attitude. He is reported to have declared that the
new budget is out of proportion to the resources of the
country.
The fact is that the nation is thoroughly committed
to a huge outlay. It is obvious to all that military ex-
pansion is responsible for the swollen budget, but few
are

and Forestry to get the appropriations for which he pleaded
at the recent Cabinet Conferences on next year's budget*
and the comparative success of the Navy Minister appear to
have contributed towards alienating the support of the
Japanese farmers from the armed services. During the early
part of the conferences the War Minister, General Araki,
kept out of the discussions and left the floor to the Fi-
Minister, en the one hand, and the Minister of Agri-

Summary:
The large sums appropriated in next year's
budget for the Army and Navy and the compa-
ratively small sums allotted for agricultural
relief threaten the previously existing en-
tente between the military and the farmers.
894.51/426
The War Office in great alarm issued a state-
ment to the press, which was supported by a
similar one issued by the Navy Office, charg-
ing that attempts were being made to antagon-
ize the farming population against the mili-
tary.
The general opinion in Tokyo is that the
statement did the military cause more harm
than good.
General Araki is now showing great concern
for the welfare of the farmers.
1
Agricultural relief is being discussed at the
Cabinet conferences on doemstic policy and the
Minister for Agriculture and Forestry hopes
that he may yet obtain the money he wants.

=== Page 328 === (confidence 1.00)
tance, such persons have been trying to make
the people regard the "crisis in 1936" as mere
propaganda of the military intended for some
ulterior purpose, telling the people that those
killed in the past all belonged to the masses
and that no high-class commanding officer has
been killed. In regard to the budget question,
such persons have been telling the people that
the farm relief question has been sacrificed for

I- The army cannot let go unchallenged this kind
of a movement to estrange the people from the
military, because it will destroy the harmonious
unity of the people, which is the foundation of
the national defense. At present, two principal
measures are being employed in the international
maneuvering for peacefully reducing the national
defence strength of any one country. One is the
anti-war movement which is carried on under ins-
tructions from the Third Internationale, and the
other is the movement to alienate the civilians
from the military.
While the former is known to all, the latter still
is known to only a few, though persistently direct-
ed against Japan from abroad through various chan-
nels and it has a great baneful influence, because
it is carried on secretly.
" Even the inclusion in the budget for the next
fiscal year of the military expenses required for
strengthening the national defense force in order
to carry out the national policy seems to be re-
garded by some persons as an excellent theme for
their maneuvering to estrange the people from the
military. This cannot be overlooked from the
standpoint of the national defense, no matter such
persons act consciously or unconsciously.
Mention is to be made of the fact that the mi-
litary expense included in the budget for the next
fiscal year represent the minimum required by the
execution of the national policy and the security
of the national defense under the existing inter-
national conditions. It is based entirely on the
consideration of the permanent security and develop-
ment of the country as well as the general welfare
of the people.
" It is a well-known fact that the army authori-
ties are seriously interested in the agricultural
community question because the question, apart
from being a matter of general national policy, is
inseparable from the matter ofnational defense.
Therefore it is a matter for profound regret both
to the national defense and the general tranquili-
ty of the nation that some persons are said to have
been trying to prejudice the agricultural communi-
ty against the military by utilizing the budget
question for their ulterior purpose."

to stand in the way of their adoption."

(b) Rice Control
On November 1 the Rice Control Act went into effect.
By this law the Government is given the p wer to set arbi-
trarily the minimum and maximum prices beyond which the
market price of rice wust not fluctuate. For the present
there is said to be no fear of the Government's being foreed
to buy up the surplus crop of the farmer at the minimum
since the price of rice is high.
However, it is expected
that the real test of the new law will come in Mareh when
the seasonal decline in the price of rice is due.

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.95)
6
Miscellaneous Pinancial Notes.
A fair improvement has been registered in the business
results of all banking institutions in this country, though
it is reported that there will be no extra distribution of
#ividends on this account for l933. The improvement re-
fleeted the general amelioration of business conditions
throughout Japan. For example the rayon output for Novem-
ber established an all-time high record and amounted to
85,486 bozes ar increase of 3,121 boxes over October.
This improvement naturally is revealed in extra ivid-
end payments, but the Ito-Chu Com erclal Compeny is believed
to be one of the few com anies in the world wh ch was able
to pay a 60% dividend last year. In other wor s in addi-

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2
expenditures of the Foreign Office. The
item, "Protection and Control of Japanese
Living Abroad," is not a new one, but I
believe that the present allotment of
1,400,000 yen is the largest on record. It
is not kmown precisely what this covers.
It used to include expenses of the Japanese
police in Manohuria and other parts of
China. However, one notices an item of
almost 4,000,000 yen for "Manchurian Affair
Expenditure," so that the additional ex-
penditures for police work in Manohuria
might well be in either appropriation.
An appropriation of 2,600,000 yen is made
for temporary allowances to the Foreign
Service, presumably to offset the de-
preciated exohange.
Under the extraordinary expenditures
of the army (page 16) you will notice an
item of 120,000,000 yen for "Repletion of
Arms," which means, of course, new equip-
ment.

BUDGET FOR 1934-35 FISCAL YEAR.
(Published in an extra issue of the OFFICIAL
GAZETTE on March 20, 1934)
Proclamation.
"We hereby sanction the general Budget and the
special account Budget for the 1934-35 fiscal year
which obtained the approval of the Imperial Diet and
cause the same to be made public."
Twentieth Day of March,
Ninth Year of Showa.
Imperial Sign Manual
& Imperial Seal
Countersignature of Prime Minister (& Minister for
Education) Viscount Makoto Saito
Other Ministers of State.
Budget
Article I. The amount of revenue and expenditure for
the 1934-35 fiscal year is fixed at Yen 2,112,133,483
each. The items of revenue and expenditure and the
amount thereof are as mentioned in the annexed General
Budget (Table One).
Article 2. The expenditures mentioned in the annexed
Budget (Table Two) are to be disbursed as Continuing
Expenditures in the 1934-35 and succeeding fiscal years
according to the specified items and amount. In some
items, however, the previously fixed total amount and
the amount of the sums allotted for specified years
have been revised.
Article 3. With regard to the expenditures mentioned
in the annexed Budget (Table Three), the balance (if
any) remaining at the end of the fiscal year (1934-35)
may be carried forward to and be appropriateu for use
in the succeeding fiscal year.
Article 4. With regard to the issue of the Treasury
Bills and borrowings to be issued or made in a ccor-
dance with the provisions of Article 6 of the Law of
Account, the maximum amount of such bills and borrow-
ings for the 1934-35 fiscal year is fixed as -
Yen 350,000,000.
Article 5. The amount of such expenditure as spreading
over the two fiscal years, for which special contract
is to be entered with the Treasury, according to the
provisions of Article II of the Law of Account, is
fixed as Yen 4,000,000 for the 1934-35 fiscal year.

V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE
No dofinite poliey has been announeed by the Ministry
of Finanee for coping with the difficult problems with
which that Department has to deal. The principal diffi-
culties to be solved are the following: the necessity
of curtailing the l934-35 budget emands of the various
Ministries in the f ce of the War and Navy Ministries'
firm insistence upon greatly increased military and
naval appropriations, which together constitute a pre-
ponderant allotment in every Japanese budget; the apparent
need, in the absence of  substantial increase in taxes,
of issuing a ditional red ink bonds, with the danger that
the saturation point may be reached in the issuance of
such bonds; and the insistent public demand for financial
relief of the impoverished farmers. The Finance Minister's
troubles have been considerably aggrav ted by the typhoon
and tidal wave which struck Osaka and its environs on
Soptember 2l and caused damages estimated at slightly
over ¥5,000,000,000 (approximetely $150,000,000). This
catastrophe will necessitate the expanding of the budget
for 1955-36 on account of the grants in aid that will be
given by the Government for the devastated districts and
also because of the loss in taxes from this section of the
country. Before the disaster the Ministry of Finance
had been counting on a natural increase in revenue of at least
 100,000,000 during the f1scal year 1934-35, but since
Osaka is the industrial center of Japan and pays & large
portion of the nation's texes, this amount of natural in-
crease in revenue will be substantially reduced. The
Government

peacefully by means of diplomacy.

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
recently made a trip to the Far East, his first and only
such trip. He does not profess previously to have been a
student of Far Eastern affairs. He spent a few weeks in
Japan and a few weeks in China. He frankly states that
he makes "no pretense of understanding the intricacies of
Japanese politics."
As contrasted with and in dissent from the opinion of
those who believe and affirm (or suggest) that Japan is at
or near the point of financial exhaustion, we have had
during the past three years many reports from our own offi-
cers and officers of the Department of Commerce located in
Japan. Most important perhaps for present purposes, we
have had from our own Embassy written reports, made under
special instruction, and based on studies concerned with
just this point, which reports were prepared by Mr. Steintorf
and Mr. Sturgeon and approved by Ambassador Grew. Mr.
Steintorf is an officer of the Department of Commerce and
has spent the last fifteen years in Japan. Mr. Sturgeon
is an officer of the Foreign Service -- a "Japan language
officer" -- and has spent the last fifteen years in Japan
and Manchuria. These two officers have recently come to
Washington and there has been put to them orally the
definite question: Do you see any signs of Japan's
"cracking" (financially) or being likely to "crack" in the
near

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
near future under the burden of her policies in the field
of foreign relations and foreign commerce? Both of them
have replied in the negative. The same question has
recently been put to the American Commercial Attaché to
China, who has spent the last thirty years in the Far East,
and to the American Minister to China, who has specialized
on the Far East during twenty-eight years of career in the
Foreign Service -- both of which officers are now in this
country -- and both of them have replied in the negative.
It was put a few weeks ago (while he was in Washington)
to the Counselor of our Embassy in Tokyo, an officer who
has spent twenty-four years in our "Japan service", and
his reply was in the negative. This question is one which
has been in the minds of officers of the Far Eastern Divi-
sion throughout the period of special stress and strain
which began in l93l, and none of these officers believe,
in relation to that question, that the facts warrant an
opinion in the affirmative.
The burden of proof with regard to an affirmation is
usually conceived of as lying with him or those who make
it. Those who believe and affirm that Japan is "cracking"
or is about to "crack" or under a little additional burden
would "crack", should submit, and in a good deal of detail,
their evidence. In such matters mere affirmations, no
matter

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
-5 -
that no one man or group of men could comprehend the
whole picture and fabric; no human being was entitled to
make with confidence such prophecies.
The same thing holds today. With regard to Japan in
partioular, anyone who undertakes to make an estimate of
what Japan can or cannot, will or will not do needs to
take into consideration the whole history of Japan, the
psychology of the Japanese people, the whole situation
both past and present in the Far East, everything that
appertains to the life and activities of Japan's neighbors,
the situation in Europe, the situation in the United States,
various combinations of facts as they are today and as they
may be tomorrow and the day after. These questions call
for consideration of much more than figures and statistics.
Without saying what Japan can or cannot do, it may
safely be affirmed that, in the state of mind which pre-
vails among them today, the Japanese people might be ex-
pected to go to almost any extreme of sacrifice and payment
in support of the present foreign policy of Japan. They
have definite ideas with regard to what they want; for it
they are willing to pay; they are ready if need be to
sacrifice their lives, they have no hesitation whatever
about sacrificing personal comfort and convenience.
Although

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Although the burden of proof does not properly
devolve upon the negative, officers of EA and FE have
made during the past month a study, based on all the
material here available, with all the consideration
that it has been possible to give that material within
so limited a period, of the evidence in support and/or
in refutation of the view under reference. The results
of this study are embodied in the memorandum which EA
submits under date November 15, covered by Dr. Feis's
memorandum of November 27, herewith. The conclusion is
reached that the evidence does not warrant an affirma-
tion or an assumption that Japan's financial and economic
structure is cracking or is about to crack or is likely
soon to be cracked in the event of (and by) the laying
on of additional (within reason) burdens.

JAPANESE FISCAL POSITION
DEC 24 1934
The Budget
8
394.51/453
As in the case of many other countries, it is not
easy to get a concise and accurate view of the condition
of the public finances of Japan. The Japanese fiscal
accounting system consists of the General Account and
of numerous Special Accounts (of which there are thirty-
four at present). These Special Acccunts bear varying
relationships to the General Account, some being main-
tained wholly independent of that Account, while in the
case of others, only the net balance is transferred to the
General Account. In other cases the relationships are
still more complex, appropriations for fixed capital
requirements being transferred from the General Account
and any net profits then being transferred back to the
General Account.
FILED
the General Accounts as published, current obligatio
F/FG
5
which are sometimes met by the delivery of bonds in lieu
of

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.96)
-
of cash are not included, and from the fact that there
are included in the current revenues the receipts from
borrowing and from the accumulated surplus transferred
from the previous year.
From this it will appear that the totals of revenue
and expenditure as published by the Department of Finance
are not of great significance except as indicating the
trends and does not show the agregate of expenditures
and revenues of the General and Special Accounts combined.
Because of the relationship of the Special Accounts to the
General Account, however, the resulting deficit or surplus
does in a fairly adequate way, however, show the results
of the current fiscal operations of the government. The
trend of the items in the General Account over the past
decade is shown in the following table:

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
4
From this table it may be seen that expenditures
continued at a relatively stable level from 1925 through
1931, that they declined somewhat in 1932, and that
beginning with the fiscal year 1932-1933 the Accounts
have been characterized by unprecedentedly large expendi-
tures accompanied by large and growing deficits necessita-
ting a very considerable augmentation of the national
debt.
It may be observed from the table that in no year
since l924 have current non-borrowed revenues equaled
the expenditures of the General Account. For the fiscal
years, 1925 to 1932, inclusive, the cumulative deficits
totalled slightly over 1,150,000 yen of which about sixty
percent was met by borrowing for the General Account, the
remainder being covered by drawing down the cash balance
(accumulated surplus) which the Government had at the
close of the 1924 fiscal year. In the two years, 1933 to
1934, expenditures have risen to an all-time high with
relatively little change in revenues with the result that
the deficit for these two years alone has totalled practi-
cally l.4 billion yen, accompanied by borrowings of like
amount. The budget for the current fiscal year contem-
plates the necessity of additional borrowings of over
three-quarters of a billion yen.

those of the state railways and the colonial Governments
which are the obligations of Japan proper. The growth

=== Page 389 === (confidence 1.00)

From this table it can be seen that the service of the
national debt has constituted in recent years somewhat
over a sixth of the total expenditures of the General
Fund with the tendency to increase despite the rapid
increase in total expenditures. This increase is even
more marked if the debt service be computed as a per-
centage of the non-borrowed revenues rather than of the
expenditures rising from about 18 1/2% in the period
1928 to 1931 to an estimated 29% in the current fiscal
year.
It should be remembered that this is only the debt
service on that part of the debt chargeable to the
General Account.
The foregoing summary of the Budget and the national
debt presents the background of the Japanese fiscal system.
It will be apparent that the Japanese Budget has had
continuous deficits since l924; that these deficits have
in recent years tended to increase very considerably; that
the non-borrowed revenues of the Government have remained
fairly

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
fairly stable and that as a consequence there has been
a very pronounced and rapid increase in the national
debt which is closely correlated with the increase in
expenditures for military purposes. It is quite clear
that no government can indefinitely continue operating
under an unbalanced budget, but for the present purposes
it is important to note that we are not concerned with
(a) the ability of the government to continue piling up
deficits indefinitely, nor (b) with the government's
ability eventually to repay the debt or meet its service.
The immediate question is: Can Japan continue its bor-
rowing policy for the immediate future, say, two to five
years? While the facts relating to the Budget and to
the debt are important as a background for an answer to
this question, it is rather clear that this is a question
primarily of the ability of the banking system to absorb
new issues of bonds and of the willingness of the govern-
ment and financial authorities to let price and exchange
levels go where they will, if necessary to carry out this
program.
Our representatives in Tokyo are of the opinion that
the Japanese Government would not attempt drastically to
increase the tax burden upon the Japanese people in the
near future in order to carry out a program of increased
expenditure. For this reason international comparisons
showing

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
-14
showing the per capita debt burden, the ratio of debt to
wealth, and the ratio of debt service to national income
in Japan and in other countries, while of possible inter-
est, are not of great importance. The question is
rather can the capital market and more especially the
banking system continue to absorb government securities
at a relatively heavy rate. The discussion in the next
section is pertinent to this question.
Relation of the Banks to Government Finance
On July 31, 1934, Japanese banks held 3.8 billion
yen of government bonds, or 42.7% of the government
debt outstanding. Another 1 2/3 billions was held by
the National Deposits Bureau as an investment of the
postal savings funds of the country. The following table
shows the relation of government bonds to the total

1932, 1933 and 1934, and the ratio of the government
debt which was held by all banks on those dates. Table
Ix shows the distribution of ownership of government
bonds at the end of the years 1932-33 and 1934 by types
of holders.

From Table VIII it may be observed that government
securities have increased from about 40% of the total
investment portfolios of ordinary banks in 1932 to around
50% in 1934; for savings banks the increase has been from
about 60% to about 67%; and for special banks from about
60% to 75% in the same period. In the United States, as
of June 30, 1934, government securities constituted about
62% of the total security investments of member banks of
the Federal Reserve System and practically loo%, of course,
of the security holdings of the Federal Reserve banks.
It would not appear, therefore, that Japanese banks are
more heavily loaded with government securities or rela-
tively less capable of absorbing additional ones than our
banks in the United States.
Neither would it appear that the Japanese public debt
is unduly concentrated in the hands of banks as compared
to the situation in the United States. On June 30, 1934,
42.8% of the 27-billion-dollar national debt was held by
all member banks and by Federal banks (as compared to the
42.7% of the Japanese debt held by all banks in Japan).
Moreover, the United States figure does not include the
holdings by non-member commercial banks and by savings
banks which, if included, would increase the percentage
above the corresponding figure for Japan.

=== Page 396 === (confidence 1.00)

From this table it will appear that although the
holdings of government securities by the Bank of Japan
have increased by something more than l00% over the
pre-l930 level, there is no evidence that the Bank has
been called upon to absorb an uncomfortably large volume
of these securities, nor that it has met undue diffi-
culty in disposing of the issues which it has floated
for the government.
The most unfavorable trend to be observed in this
summary of the position of the Bank of Japan is the
rather heavy decline in the Bank's gold holdings. These
figures of gold coin and bullion, however, are perhaps an
inadequate picture of Japan's gold position inasmuch as
they apparently represent only the gold held against note
issues. In 1929 the Japanese Government ceased to publish
its holdings of gold at the Bank of Japan and abroad.
Further, to gather an adequate picture of the reserve
position of the banking system of Japan, it would be
necessary to include the holdings of foreign exchange
which are largely concentrated in the Yokohama Specie
Bank.
Aside from the gold holdings, the other items in
Table X do not indicate either any inability of the Bank
to continue to finance the government nor do the figures
on

=== Page 397 === (confidence 1.00)
-20
on notes issued and total deposits indicate that there
has been any necessity for recourse to inflation of
central bank credit as a method of financing the govern-
ment to date, nor does it indicate any reason for believ-
ing that in the relatively near future such an inflation
would be called for by the continued issuance of govern-
ment securities.
Apparently the only, or at least the main, point
upon which any prediction of the relatively immediate
embarrassment of the Bank of Japan could be based lies
in its gold position; but as pointed out above the true
gold position of Japan as a unit is not known; and as
is pointed out below in the section on the balance of
payments it does not seem likely that Japan would be
faced by an embarrassing drain of payments abroad, even
if the government were to attempt to prevent a further
depreciation of the yen in the case that such a drain
did materialize.
Further evidence of the government's ability to
finance a further expansion of the debt may be gained
from the record of the yield of government securities
on the domestic bond market. The yield on government
bonds declined from an average of 6% in 1925 to only
slightly over 5% in 1929, and then gradually rose to
5 1/2% in 1932. Despite the very considerable increases
in

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
-21
in bond flotations in the years beginning l932, however,
the banking system has been able to maintain a relatively
easy money market with the result that the average yield
on government bonds has rather steadily declined from an
average of 5 1/2% in 1932 to about 4 1/2% so far in 1934.
At the same time the yield on all bondshas shown a
corresponding decline, falling from over 6% in 1932 to
around 5% at the present time. These figures are perhaps
indicative only of the fact that a government can with
the cooperation of the central banking system maintain a
relatively easy money market when it is to its advantage
to do so. It does indicate, however, when coupled with
the fact that the Bank of Japan's holdings of government
bonds have not increased, that the ability of the Japanese
banking system to continue to absorb government offerings
has not yet been seriously impaired.
Production and Price Indices
Two further measures of the internal economy of Japan
are to be found in the wholesale price index and in pro-
duction indices. As indicated in the tables below, these
indices show only a rather rapid advance in productive
activity in Japan as compared to the depression levels in
the United States and in the rest of the world, but do
not show any indications of an internal crack-up in Japan.
Particularly

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.98)
-22
Particularly interesting are the indices of production of
investment and consumption goods, which show a much
greater relative improvement since 1930 in the production
of investment goods than in the production of consumption
goods - a type of improvement which it is universally
agreed is vital for a return to a sound prosperity. These
indices, if they show anything, seem to show only that
Japan has achieved a relatively more satisfactory position
that has most of the rest of the world.

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)

International Balance of Payments
The foregoing sections seem to indicate that in-
ternally the Japanese economy is quite capable of sustain-
ing additional burdens within reason. A program such as
is contemplated, however, would undoubtedly require a
certain dependence on the outside world for certain
resources which Japan does not possess within the Empire.
There remains to inquire, therefore, what is the condition
of the Japanese balance of international payments. The
following tables present in summary form the development
of the balance of payments over the past nine years. The
summary table has been prepared in the Department and,
while fairly adequate as indicating trends, may be slightly
inaccurate , particularly in the last two years. The
comparative tables published under the League of Nations
classification unfortunately come down only through 1931
and it has been necessary to construct the tables for l932
and l933 from various sources, and in order to secure
comparability these tables have been carried back to 1925.
Table XIII

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.99)

=== Page 408 === (confidence 1.00)
-31-
Certain aspects of the balance of payments are
particularly noteworthy.
1. Except for 1929 and 1932 Japan (including the
colonies) has had a continuously unfavorable balance on
current account, but a balance which has become decidedly
less unfavorable with a tendency toward an even balance
since 1929.
2. The negative trade balance, which at the outset
of this period amounted to over 350 million yen per year,
has been steadily declining and at its present rate might
soon become positive.
3. The adverse current balance down through 1928
was partially offset by an inflow of long-term capital
into Japan. Since 1929, however, there has been a
relatively heavy net outflow of capital from Japan. A
glance at the detailed figures indicates that in no
small part this outflow is probably to be ascribed to a
rapid repatriation of Japanese securities, including the
retirement of the external debt at a rate undoubtedly
more rapid than is called for by the sinking fund pro-
visions of those issues.
4. Net specie movements have been relatively
small in the period under consideration, except for the
years 1930 to 1932, when there was a heavy outflow.
5.

=== Page 409 === (confidence 1.00)
-32-
5. There remains relatively large amounts in the
unaccounted for balance, which since l930 have been
positive, except for 1933. In addition to the usual
errors and omissions, possibly including short-term
capital movements, these may indicate a piling up of
foreign balances which are not accounted for in the
detailed accounts.
6. Throughout the period covered by this table
there is indicated a very definitely improving trend in
the Japanese balance of payments, among the most favorable
of which may be mentioned again (a) the trend in the
merchandise trade balance which, if continued, will not
unlikely become active instead of passive, which, ccupled
with a continuing positive balance on current invisi-
bilities, hould convert the negative balance on current
account into a favorable balance; (b) upon necessity the
outward flow of capital undoubtedly could be much re-
duced, giving rise to rather large Japanese claims upon
the rest of the world for goods or services.
In summary there seems to be no reason for conclud-
ing that the external position of Japan for the immediate
future is likely to be any more unfavorable than the
internal, and in neither does there appear any reason
for assuming that Japan could not continue to finance
relatively heavy expenditures if, for considerations of
national policy, she so desired to do.

(5) "Our taxation, both national and local, amounts to
12 per cent of the income of our people, as compared
with 25 per cent in England, 14 per cent in America,
21 per cent in France, etc." (Kawakami)
The Division of Finance and Investments, Bureau
of Foreign and Domestic Commerce, and the Division of
Research and Statistics, Treasury Department, both
advised that it is impossible to get figures scientific-
ally worked out in regard to the percentage of taxation
in relation to national income. Any figures are
naturally unreliable as the calculations for national
income and local taxation, both of which are rapidly
changing, are in most instances nothing more than
estimates.

are changing unusually rapidly and late national income
figures are not readily available; that the Treasury
cannot give out any information on finances of foreign
governments; but that, CONFIDENTIALLY AND UNOFFICIALLY,

States (14%) were approximately correct in 1931, that
France's percentage (21%) of taxation to national

Kawakami had had recently, in Japan, with high Japanese
officials, among them Viscount Saito, Admiral Okada,
Admiral Osumi, Admiral Nomura, General Masaki, and
Viscount Ishii. It was written in the form of questions
and answers. The concluding question and its answer are
of partioular interest in conneotion with the question
of Japan's economio and financial situation and her
"ability to pay." This question and answer read as
F/F
follows:
G
"QURSTION: Do you anticipate building
rivalry should the present naval conversations
at London end in failure? Should this rivalry
come about, would Japan be financially pre-
pared to cope with it?
JAN 16 1935
TILED
"ANSTER: We can not read the minds of
other powers, but as far as we are concermed,
we can not conceive of any suoh situation as
will oblige us to embark upon reokless and
unnecessary building eompetition. Certainly

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
we will do nothing to provoke such a situation.
No two nations, such as America and Japan,
having no problems between them which can not
be amicably solved through diplomacy, would
build ships just for the sake or buiiding. But
should such a rivalry unfortunately develop, we
are confident of our ability to cope with it.
We recognize the unhappy economic condition of
our farmers, but at the same time, remember
these faots:
"(1) We are not going to build big and
costly ships, no matter what America or England
may build.
"(2) Our national income per capita
increased from 58 yen in 1914 to 165 yen in
1932, while that of America decreased from
$351 to $311 in the same period.
"(3) Our import and export trade inoreased
from 1,430,000,000 yen in 1914 to 2,982,000,000
yen in l932, while that of America decreased from
$4,276,000,000 t0 $2,935,000,000.
"(4) Our national debts in 1932 totaled
8,139,000,000 yen, as compared with America's
22,538,000,000 exc1us1ve of the $4,000,000,000
emergenoy loans raised by the American Government
last year.
"(5) Our taxation, both national and local,
amounts to l2 per cent of the income of our
people, as compared with 25 per cent in England,
14 per cent in America, 2l per cent in France,
eto.
"(6) Our exports shrank from l00 in the
peak year of 1929 to only 87 in 1933; England's
shrank to 50; America's to 32; France's to 37;
Germany's to 29.
"Roughly, the same ratio of shrinkage was seen
in the imports of the leading nations.
"All things considered, our financial and
economic position is more favorable than those of
other nations. But why stress suoh facts? We
anticipate no such naval rivalry as may cause us
serious financial embarrassment."

(a) Foreign Trade.

According to the JAPAN TI ES of April 16, on the
previous day a plan of encouraging imports into Japan
from Central and South American countries was decided
upon at a conference of officials of the Foreign Office
and the Dopartment of Commerce and Industry and representa-
tives of various business organizations wheroby financial
assistance wili be given to Japanese importers who may
use the money thus advanced for pronoting increased
imports from the countries concerned.
Apparently the
fund required for this purpose is to be obtained from the
control fee now levied by the Exporters' Association on
Japanese exports to the countries of CeTtral and South
America.
It is mderstood by this newepaper that Japan

from El Salveder
e the same tim esteblishing an

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The total national budget for the fiscal year 1936-37
is expected by Rengo and Nippon Dempo (news agencies)
to reach ¥2,900,000,000 or about ¥710,000,000 more than
894.51
the current budget. As in former years, the Minister for
Finance has stated that he intends to scale down the
demands of the Ministries so that the budget for the next
fiscal year will not exceed that of the current year.
On July 26 Mr. Takahashi issued a statement on
government finance in which he refuted the view that the
issuance of deficit bonds over an indefinite period
would have no bad effect and added that excessive
issuance of such bonds would lead to the ruin of finance
and economy. The ASAHI of August 1 quoted the Finance
Minister as having stated on the last day of July that
out of consideration for the delicacy of international
relations he would recognize indispensable appropriations
for national defense but that he was not in favor of
approving unnecessary defense measures which were likely
to cause international competition in arms and which
might make Japan "hated by the world".
There is little indication in the above-mentioned
ambiguous statoment of the Finance Minister that he
entertains any serious desire to engage in a struggle

GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Shortly after the withdrawal of the Jananese delegation
S
from the London Naval Conference, Mr. Takahashi, the Finance
Minister, made a statement to the press that the failure of
the conference was not an event that need lead to any marked
increase in Japan's naval armaments and warranted no general
increase in taxation. In his sneech before the House of
Representatives on January 21 Mr. Takahashi announced that
the national budget for the fiscal year 1936-37 had been
balanced with an income and expenditure of ¥2,278,000,000,
which represented an increase cver the fiscal year 1935-36
of ¥62,000,000; stated that the international situation
demanded a large expenditure for national defense; declared
that there must be no decrease in the appropriation for
public welfare, owing to the impoverished state of the
rural districts; remarked on the steady increase in Japan's
foreign trade; and expressed gratification over the closer
economic ties with "Manchukuo".
In a New Year messace issued through the Rengo News
Agency, Baron Seinosuke Goh, President of the Tokyo Chamber
of Commerce and Industry, stated that there were already
signs that absorption of Government bonds would not be so
easy

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.99)
-19 -
easy in the future; thet due to the Government's lack of a
financial policy for the future uncertainty had already
begun to be felt over the financial situation; and that
to remove this uncertainty it would be necessary for the
Government to establish a firm policy. Less pessimistic
views on the financial situation were attributed by the
Jananese press to Mr. Fukai, Governor of the Bank of Japan,
who was quoted as having said in the early part of the month
that in his belief economic activity would become even
brisker in the new year; that this expansion of activity
would naturally have great bearing on the money market;
that a crisis would come only when public confidence was
lost in the Government's bond pclicy; and that it was
unthinkable that public confidence would be lost in the
near future:

through a raise in taxation, improvement and expansion

In a conversation with the Embassy's Commercial

for Finance, stated that he was afraid that some people in
the United States had gained the impression that the
February 26 incident was a sort of revolution; that this
idea was not correct, since the real cause of the disturb-
ance was the long-existing defects in Government policies
relating to industry and finance and since the purpose of
the insurrection was to bring about much-needed reforms;
that the present Government realized the need of such
reforms but expected to bring them about gradually. Mr.

would be increased and added that some form of increased

taxation would be put into effect, that if possible red
ink bonds would be reduced, but that it would be impossible

that the foreign exchange policy of the late Mr. Takahashi
would be followed by the present régime, namely, the main-

and 10.* All in all, it may be said that the statements
that
mentioned above indicate the new Government plans definitely
to depart from the Takahashi financial policy by gradual
steps.
VI.
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for March amounted to ¥215,473,000
and its imports were ¥276,019,000, as compared with February
exports of ¥208,420,000 and imports of ¥283,305,000. These
figures represented an unfavorable balance of trade of
¥60,546,000 as against an unfavorable balance for February
of ¥74,885,000.**
(b). Financial Trends.
The stock and bond markets were weak owing to the
prevailing uncertainty over the new Government's future
financial and economic policy. The average yen selling
rate was $0.2895, i. e., slightly lower than the average
rate for February of 0.290l.**

from the Embassy at London, England.
COPY OF AN ARTICLE FROM THE STATIST, JUNE 20, 1936.
JAPAN IN SUSPENSE
Our Tokyo Correspondent writes under date of May 27: While
Japan's armament boom promises even further expansion her
export boom gradually seems to be declining, and the depression
in those branches of industry which cater for the civilian home
market tends to become more pronounced. The uncertainties which
exist in regard to the new Government's forthcoming "reform"
policies rather add to the feeling of suspense which at present
prevails in Japanese business circles.
The one thing which is certain is that armament expenditure
will be rapidly increased once more, beginning with the fiscal
year 1937-38. Both Army and Navy seem to be working on Five-
Year or perhaps Six-Year Plans, by which their strength is to be
enhanced to a very large extent. The greater part of such
further expenditure, which might raise the total budget by more
than half, will have to be covered by further borrowing. The
new Minister of Finance, Dr. Eiichi Baba, who professes to be
much less orthodox and also less afraid of the consequences
which might follow inflationary measures than was his prede-
cessor, has threatened to resort to the compulsory placing of
future Government bonds in the event of the financial community
failing to continue absorbing such issues as capital investment.
Meanwhile Dr. Baba has begun forcing down money rates in order
to facilitate the placing of such bonds in the future. The
whole range of money rates was recently lowered as much as from
o.1 to O.4 per cent. At the same time, the Bank of Japan was
ordered to discontinue temporarily all further sales of Govern-
mont bonds from its own holdings, and in a short interval two
conversions of 5 per cent. Government bonds into 32 per cent.
issues were announced. Further action to the same effect has
been/

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
been to prepare the money market not only for greater willing-
ness to absorb the forthcoming emission of deficit bonds, but
also for a revival of the investor's interest in issues required
to finance a further extension of the armament industries.
The recent development of Japan's export trade is rather
unsatisfactory. This is especially true of cotton piece goods,
which form by far the most important single item in Japanese
sales abroad. During the first quarter of the current year,
exports of all kinds of cotton piece goods amounted only to 636,-
000,000 yards, compared with 714,000,000 yards during the fi rst
quarter of 1935. Only grey cloth, in which there seems to be
the least profit, showed a small increase, while exports of
bleached and specially finished cloth dropped to an unexpeotedly
large extent. Among the other export goods which showed a
deerease during the period were silk tissues, pottery, paper and
caustic soda. Even rayon tissues and toys barely held their own,
while raw silk, machinery, miscellaneous iron manufactures and
tinned comestibles were about the only groups which showed an
increase in export values. The total result of the first quar-
ter's export trade, in comparison with the corresponding period
of 1935, was a small rise from 561 to 584 million yen. The
great advance of recent years seems to have come to a standstill.
Import trade grew much more, i.e., from 7il million pen to
773 million, mostly on aocount of raw material imports directly
or indirectly connected with armament activities. The Yokohama
Specie Bank compiles a set of index figures to show the compara-
tive development of the value and volume of Japan's foreign trade.
These figures reveal results which look rather discouraging.
In order to import roughly 30 per cent. more than in l932 of raw
materials and other goods needed from abroad, Japan had to
increase the volume of her own exports of manufactured articles
by/

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
by more than 53 per cent. ▲ growing proportion of her
imports consisted of raw materials in their crudest state,
while in her total exports highly finished manufactured
articles came to play an increasingly important role. It
seems therefore that as far as intrinsic values are concerned
7
the expansion in the turnover of Japan's foreign trade must
involve a certain national loss. The reason is that as an
importer Japan has to put up with world prices which she can
hardly influence, while as an exporter she tries to sustain
or even further extend the value of her total shipments by
foroing down her own prices and so disprcportionately in-
creasing the volume of her exports.
The unfavorable balance of Japan's foreign trade tends
to grow even with regard to money values. During the period
from January 1 to May 20 of the current year the import
excess amounted to 268,000,000 yen, as against 194,000,000
during the corresponding period of last year. Though this
is the main importing season, which is usually followed by a
certain excess of exports in the latter part of the year,
financial circles feel rather uneasy about the final results
to be expected for this year. This is one of the reasons why
there seems to be more apprehension than there has been for
a long time, about the possibility of further depreciation
of the yen. If this should not occur the difficulties of
Japan's export trade are bound to grow, for in such a case the
advantage of a depreciated rate of exchange, which played such
a large role in the success of Japanese sales abroad, might
gradually disappear. According to the Bank of Japan the index
of Japanese wholesale prices is now higher by about 23 per
cent. than it was in the year before Japan left the gold stan-
dard, and about 5 per cent. higher than at the same time last
year.
The/

=== Page 452 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The continuous rise in the cost of living, which has
been accelerated in recent months, is also & matter of
concern. It amounted to 7.6 per cent. during the last
twelve months and to 18 per cent. since Japan's departure
from the gold standard. But during the same period wage
rates have decreased by l.5 per cent. and 1l.4 per cent.
respectively, so that real wages have already fallen re-
markably, even though they were always very low indeed.
The fall in real wages may continue for some time and thus
help Japan to carry on her export campaign, but, on the
other hand, it must serve to restrict domestic purchasing power
more and more, thus intensifying the depression of many branches
of business. This is where the problem of inflation comes in.
The new Government which was established after the
military uprising in February last is bound by its promise
to improve social conditions in the country and to make the
wheels of inaustry turn quicker. This cannot be done by
armaments alone. If the Government wants to fulfil its
promise and thereby obviate some of the fundamental causes
which led to the February revolt, it must do something sub-
stantial in order to relieve agriculture as well as the
small manufacturing and trading communities (which seem to
be suffering even more from Japan's breathless exertions in
both the armament and export fields than do the workers
in the larger-scale industrial enterprises). Such a policy,
however, would entail additional budget expenditure in one
form or another, and that on top of increased spending for
armament purposes.
New taxes could hardly do much to provide the additional
revenue required--neither could the fundamental reform of
financial policy whioh has been announced for some later date,
together with an intended increase in taxation. It seems,
therefore, that the Government has to choose between two
alternatives; either an active policy of liberal spending
(not/

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
(not only for armaments, but also for relief and general
stimulation purposes) which would have certain inflationary
consequences; or a passive polioy of retrenchment with
regard to all civilian work, in favour of higher armament
expenditure, which would, no doubt, have the consequence of
further inoreasing social tension. The Government is in an
embarrassing situation. The Army, which is at present the
strongest force behind the Hirota Cabinet, has committed
it to the first alternative. But resistance in the camp
of Big Business, and fear among Government circles themselves
lest such an inflationary policy might become uncontrollable,
are so great that for the time being at least the second
alternative seems to have been adopted as the practical policy
of the Government. This is why it finds itself already
attacked by the same kind of activist criticism that used to
be levelled against the "impotent and inactive" Okada Govern-
ment which was swept away by the recent military revolt.

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.96)
August 5 1936
