ANDOS
Department of state
Dear Mr. Secretary:
I have for acknowledgment your communication of lugust 5, l936,
with inclosed copy of an article forwarded by our Fmbassy at London
from THE STATIST of June 20, entitled, JAPAN IN SUSPENSI. You request
such comment upon its contents as the Acting Secretary of Commerce mey
desire to make.
894.51/471
Upon the whole this article impresses us as embodying the usual
run of arguments to be found in current issues of the Japanese press.
There are no exceptional feetures apperent to sugest that its con-
clusions ere formed from any exceptional study of Japan's economic
position. On the other hand, emphtsis is placed on certain obvious
and logical trends in that position which meke it appear rather more
precarious than may be warrented.
Exports - While it is true that exports of cotton and silk piece
goods have fallen off, the declines are offset by gains in exports of
sundry goods on which import restrictions by foreign countries are not
so severe. The fact that exports are not leaping forward at the same
rate as in the past two years is not necessarily & sign of disaster.
In fact it could hardly have been expected that they should continue
to maintain such a rate of increase. Japanese business profits, how-
ever, have been exceptionally large over the past few years, and
profits could still be showm even if export returns fell short of the
records set in 1934 or 1935. At the present writing, however, they are
above the comparable period of 1935.
Imports - Stress is placed on the heavy imports during the first
part of the year, "mostly on account of raw materials directly or in-
directly connected with armament activities". Examination of the
official trade statistics, however, show that most of the increase has
resulted from larger imports of wool, probably as the result of buyers
rushing to lay in stocks in anticipation of the boycott of Australien
wool. It is our understanding that stocks of wool on hand and enroute
to Japan (not subject to license), will take care of requirements for
the remainder of this year. The wool is not necessarily used for
"armament activities", as Japan has built up a woolen industry of her
F/FG
owm mhich supplies a substantial export market as well as domestic needs
in woolen goods.

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Wages - The article bases the decline in wages and purchasing power
on a drop in real wages. Its figures of decline in wage rates are substan-
tially in accordance with labor indices in this office. But the decline in
wage rates does not mean a like drop in actual earnings. On the contrary,
the same labor index shows a small gain in actual earnings between 1931 and
1935, the index number for 1931 standing at 90.7 and for 1935 at 91.1 (based
on 100 as of 1926). Thus the argument that "low wages must serve to re-
strict domestic purchasing power more and more, thus intensifying the de-
pression of many branches of business", and threatening to bring about in-
flation is plausible but may not be sound. Mr. Burton Crane, a well known
American writer in Japan on economic subjects, is of the opinion, as ex-
pressed in a recent article in the JAPAN ADVERTISER, that the threat of
inflation is more likely to come from the Government's present policy of
practically compelling the banks to take more and more of its bonds until
it may be that the Government will have to buy back the same bonds when
the banks find themselves so overloaded that they will have to release some
part of them. Moreover, while the purchasing power of the industrial
workers may be said to have declined in the sense that the gain in actual
earnings does not come anywhere near approximating the increase in living
costs, the farmers, on the other hand, have had their purchasing power in-
creased in the last year and a half, owing to higher silk and rice prices.
Of course the policies, both financial and economic, of the present
Government appear so unsettled that it is difficult for anyone to hazard
a guess as to where Japan is headed or how seriously her economy may be
threatened. We are inclined, however, to feel with Trade Commissioner
Paul P. Steintorf, Tokyo, whose report of July 6 contained the following
comments:
"Personally, I can see no evidence of real inflation or of
serious strain to the financial or economic structure. On the contrary,
finance, trade, and industry appear to be sound and healthy at the
present time -— although the future is somewhat clouded by the certainty
of a marked increase in Government expenditures (and consequently in
the issue of deficit bonds), and by Japan's heavy financial commit-
ments in Manchuria..
"Up to the present, Japan's trade has been extremely flexible,
gains in old markets having been balanced by the development of new
outlets, while restrictions on established commodities have been off-
set by the development of new products. It is somewhat doubtful
whether this trend can be carried much further, for there are few new
markets left to exploit and even in the newer markets, such as South
Africe, restrictions are beginning to be evident. Similarly, there
are not a great number of new products upon which a large export
business can be developed.
"There can be no doubt that Japan is firmly convinced that the
country's economic life depends entirely upon continued expansion of
industry and steady gains in exports of manufactured goods, since

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.94)
-3-
only in this way can she provide a livelihood for her steadily in-
creasing population. I believe that she will use peaceful means to
obtain her objective, but any marked decline in export trade, either
because of foreign restrictions or other causes, will be considered as
a national calamity sufficiently grave to justify the use of her
military power."
Sincerely yours,
X

I have the honor to acknowleage the receipt of the
47
Departnent's instruction 1.0. 1094,/ dated August 5, 1936,
with which was trinsmitted a copy of en article entitled
"Japan in Sus ense", which was published in the STATIST on
June 30, 1936. The Department requests the conments of
the Embassy with regard to the article under reference.
The principal point in the article would appear to be
contained in the last paragraph, wherein it is stated that
"It
11

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
64-2
- 2 -
"It seems, therefore, that the Government
(the Japanese Government) has to choose be-
tween two alternatives; either an active policy
of liberal spending(not only for armaments, but
also for relief and general stimulation purposes)
which would have certain inflationary consequences;
or a passive policy of retrenchment with regard to all
civilian work, in favour of higher armament expen-
diture, which would, no doubt, have the consequence
of further increasing social tension . . . . But re-
sistance in the camp of Big Business, and fear among
Government circles themselves lest such an inflationary
policy might become uncontrollable, are so great that
for the time being at least the second alternative
seems to have been adopted as the practical policy
of the Government."
In commenting upon this statement, it would seem to be
advisable first briefly to review the present financial
condition of Japan, in order to endeavor to estimate to what
extent the Japanese Government can carry out its pledges in
regard to social reforms, in addition to meeting the expen-
ditures being forced upon it for military expansion.
At the end of August, 1936, the Japanese national debt ,
exclusive of rice notes and short term treasury notes, amount-
ed to Yen 9,889,472,000, of which Yen 1,328,000,000 con-
sisted of bonds issued in foreign currencies and Yen 8,56l,472
of bonds issued within Japan. Within the next few days, the
Government will issue deficit, or "red ink", bonds to the
amount of Yen 250,000,000. The total outstanding national
debt (exclusive of rice notes and short term treasury notes,
which amount to about half a billion yen), therefore, will
exceed ten billion yen by the end of September, l936. At
the end of March, l93l, the national debt, exclusive of rice
notes and short term treasury notes, amounted to Yen 5,955,816.00
There has therefore been an increase of the national debt of
over four billion yen in the past five and one-half years.
Even so, however, the debt is not excessive today. During
this period, the yen has been depreciated, in comparison
with currencies based on gold values, by 40 per cent. In
1931,

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.98)
64-3
- 3 -
1931, the per capita national debt was Yen 60.59, or
$29.99. At the present time, taking the population of the
Japanese Empire at about 95 millions, the per capita
national debt amounts to Yen 106.75, or (at the present
rate of exchange) $31.49. There has, therefore, been an
increase of only about $1.50 per capita in the Japanese
4.5.
national debt, notwithstanding an increase of over 4 billion
yen in the total debt.
At the same time the fact must be considered that
a yen is still a yen to the Japanese and that the per
capita debt, considered in yen, is Yen 46.16, or 76 per
cent,higher than it was in 1931. Since 1931 the cost
of living in Japan has risen, but not nearly in the same
proportion as the yen has been depreciated. The fact must
also be taken into consideration, however, that by means
of closing the open market for Japanese domestic bonds
since April 3, 1936, the Goverrment has been able to refund
some of its 5 per cent issuesinto 3 1/2 per cent issues,
thus decreasing the interest burden on the nation.
In the present fiscal year the Japanese Government must,
according to the budget, issue deficit bonds to the amount
of Yen 518,000,000, of which Yen 250,000,000, or the first
issue, are to be sold next week. In addition to the
deficit bonds, the Government will issue, according to the
budget, special bonds (for power and other projects in
Japan Proper and in the colonies) amounting to Yen 266,000,000.
The total amount of bonds to be issued by the Japanese
Government during the present fiscal year, which ends
March 31, 1937, therefore, is Yen 784,000,000.
Expenditures

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
64-4
- 4 -
Expenditures for the present fiscal year will
amount to about Yen 2,300,000,000, while the revenue of
the Government is estimated at about Yen 1,782,000,000.
During the next taxable year, owing to the natural in-
crease of revenue and the revenue to be derived from
additional taxes yet to be levied, it is expected that
the Government income will increase by about Yen 300,000,000.
The revenue of the Government during the 1937-38 fiscal
year can therefore be estimated at somewhat over Yen 2,000,000,000
Against this, the press estimates the next budget at about
Yen 3,200,000,000 (subject, of course, to the usual paring
down). The defense services will demand, according to the
press, over Yen 1,500,000,000 (Yen 822,000,000 for the Army
and Yen 770,000,000 for the Navy), while the ordinary ex-
penditures of the Government outside the defense services
wil1 require about Yen 1,500,000,000 and new projects of
governmental departments other than the defense services
wil1 require from Yen 170,000,000 to Yen 200,000,000. Even
if the revenue of the Government increases at the rate ex-
pected, therefore, the income will be short of expenditures
by from Yen 1,000,000,000 to Yen 1,200,000,000. The problem
for the next fiscal year thus resolves itself into the quest-
ion of whether or not the Japanese nation will be able to
absorb the large amount of deficit bonds to be issued during
the year.
The Minister of War, General Count Terauchi, was reported
in the newspapers to have stated that the Japanese nation's
power of absorption of bonds is almost limitless and that the
country could easily absorb bonds to the value of Yen
10,000,000,000, or more if necessary. The Minister for
Finance

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.99)
64-5
- 5 -
Finance, Mr. Baba, promptly retorted that the nation could,
according to his present calculations of the increase of
wealth, absorb deficit bonds to the amount of only Yen
1,000,000,000 per annum. The opinion of foreign bankers
of Tokyo is that, should the present industrial prosperity
continue, and the rate of increase of foreign trade
not drop appreciably, the estimate of r. Baba is appro-
ximately correct. In other words, the capital of the
country will increase at the rate of about Yen 1,000,000,000
per annum and the country can therefore absorb at most that
amount in deficit bonds each year. But, the bankers state,
that estimate does not take into consideration the large
investments which Japan is making in Manchuria and the
further investments which they will presumably be called
upon to make in North China. Moreover, the bankers state,
the Jananese will soon have to consider the redemption or
issued
refunding of the large amount of bonds abroad by the elec-
tric power companies. The total increase of capital in
Japan, therefore, will not be available for the absorption
of deficit bonds, and it is possible that such absorption
will become more difficult as time goes on.
According to the press, the Army will demand Yen
3,300,000,000, in addition to its usual operating expen-
ditures, during the next six years, for its replenishment
program. The first installment of Yen 300,000,000 is in-
cluded in the Army budget of Yen 822,000,000 referred to
above, while, in each of the next five fiscal years, the
Army wil1 demand Yen 600,000,000. The Navy requires, ac-
cording to the newspapers, Yen 1,000,000,000 in the next five
years for its reconstruction program. It would appear,
therefore,

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.99)
64-6
- 6 -
therefore, if these defense programs are carried out,
that still larger budgets than that for the 1937-38
fiscal year will be required for the succeeding years.
While, therefore the Hirota Cabinet has included
in its budget for the 1937-38 fiscal year new projects
of ministries other than the defense ministries (pro-
jects designed to assist in the stabilization of the
livelihood of the people and to promote the industrial
and conmercial welfare of the nation) totaling Yen
170,000,000 to Yen 200,000,000, it is doubtfu1 if such
projects can be carried in succeeding budgets because of the
rapidly mounting defense demands and the natural limi-
tation on the amount ofdeficit bonds which can be issued.
Mr. Hirota, therefore, may in the future be in the anoma-
lous position of being unable to carry out the general
reforms demanded by the military at the time of the or-
ganization of his Cabinet, primarily because of the financial
demands made by the defense services themselves.
The above digest of the present and probable future
financial position of Japan, however, is based on the
presumption that the yen will retain its present value.
Mr. Baba has stated, in a newspaper interview, that it may
become necessary to force out the deficit bonds by re-
quiring banks, trust companies, and insurance companies
to purchase them. Should the Finance Department adopt
some such plan, heavy inflation of the currency will al-
most certainly follow. Inflation to a small degree ac-
companies each bond issue but this inflation to date has
taken place very slowly and in an orderly manner. If
bonds

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.98)
64-7
- 7 -.
bonds should be förced on the public in large amounts,
inflation will become more rapid, the price level will
rise and the export trade will be affected. This will
cause a fall in the exchange value of the yen which will
result in a further depreciation of its value in terms of
gold. The bonds issued, therefore, will not have the in-
trinsic value which they had when they were issued. In
other words, large deficit bond issues forced upon the
people will amount to a levy on capital.
The Embassy and the foreign bankers in Tokyo have
no information as to the policy which will be adopted
by the Government in this connection. If the policy
of forcing out large bond issues, with the risk of heavy
inflation and further depreciation of the yen, should be
adopted by the Government, it would be possible for the
Hirota Cabinet to carry out both the military expansion
demanded by the Army and Navy and the general reform
schemes to which the Cabinet was pledged at the time of
its inauguration. The indications are that the Army is
more or less in favor of heavy bond issues, inflation
and depreciation of the yen, but that Mr. Baba is doing
his best to avoid such steps. According to the news-
papers, the Army had a plan for the expenditure of Yen
10,000,000,000 for replenishment of national defense,
to be expended over a period of six years, but it was
pared down to Yen 3,300,000,000 through the efforts of
Mr. Baba.
Respectfully yours

(407 fr.75) for each bond and for forty
(40) matured coupons."
The communiqué further indicated that the
General Assembly of French Bondholders will soon
be convoked to consider a compromise arrangement.
The London Financial Times of October 27, 1936,
published the following details of the offer of
the City of Tokio:
" Firstly, the City of Tokio will pay
cash in French francs for all redeemed bonds
and all matured interest coupons whatever
the date of maturity or of redemption.
" Secondly, the City of Tokio will
deliver for all bonds drawn or not at each
of the periodical drawings a bond of the
face value of Frs. 407.75, bearing interest
at 5 per cent per annum and redeemable in
30 years.
" Thirdly, the City of Tokio will
deliver for every 40 interest coupons which
have matured a similar 5 per cent bond.
" Fourthly, at the moment of estimating
the amount of the deposits which the City
of Tokio may be called upon to make in
France in view of constituting the necessary
provision for the payment in cash of the
bonds which have been drawn and the coupons
whioh have matured there shall be taken into
account the amount already paid as well as
the sums already available and the amounts
guaranteed on legal seizure or distraint
warrants."

THE SOUTH GHINA MORNING POST
Lons Kons. Oetober 17, 1956.
SOUTHWARD MOVE
Loans to 15. 000 Japamse in Malaye and N. .I.
As the first step towards its "southward" business
poliey, the Bank of Formosa is to offer special loans to
Japanese merehants, farmers and industrialists in Malaya
and the Nethe rlanus Indles.
There are over 15,000 Japanese in these countries,
mostly working on rub ber estates or engaged in farming
or merehandising. Because of the prolonged depresai on,
however, they are all badly off. Many merehants in the
interior are in fact on the verge of bankruptey owing
to the failure of wholesale merchants in Singapore and
elsewhere.
The Bank is now offering a maximm of $2,000 per
capite in Singapore end 2.000 guilders in Java. The
Interest rates will be fixed at rates lower than those
ruling in the respeotive districts. No mortgages will
be required in case there are guar antors. Loans will
be repayable in one year.
It 1s stated that the Bank has earmarked 500,000
guildors for Jave end $250,000 for Sing apore.

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.99)
U -2- #5, Sec. 1, Jan. 13, noon from Tokyo
(A). The Government has experienced difficulties in
disposing of the issues of 31% deficit bond of the present
fiscal year and in November the market price fell below
the issuing price.
(B). The greatly increased budget for the 1937-38
fiscel year will require the issuance of nearly one billion
yen additional deficit bonds and it will probably be
necessary for the government to exert pressure on the
banks, trust companies, et cetera, to compel them to buy
the 1937-38 issues.
(C). The banks are unable to dispose of the bonds
to the public as fast as they acquire liabilities in
respect to the bonds which makes for dangerous credit in-
flation.
(D). It appears likely that the import excess for 1937
will be larger than in 1936 as imports of raw materials
will increase because of the demands of the munitions and
allied industries while Japanese exports are meeting in-
creased opposition abroad. This will bring further pres-
sure on the yen.
(E). The Yokohama specie bank does not publish the
amount of its foreign exchange reserves abroad but foreign
bankers in Tokyo are of the opinion that those reserves
(formerly three or four hundred million yen) have been
aeriously impaired through efforts in 1936 to maintain the
yom at one ahilling and two pence. It will therefore be
increasingly

One. The present financial situation in Japan is less
51/475
favorable than it has been for some years past but conditions
have not as yet become serious. The national debt rose to
well over ten billion yen at the end of l936 (over
eleven billion yen if the external debt is calculated at
present exchange rates) but this amount does not appear to
be excessive for a nation of the size and power of Japan.
Money rates ar easy. There has been little or no currency
inflat ion and the note issue today (yen one billion four
FILED
hundred and thirty eight million) is covered to about
66% by the specle reserve. The visible foreign trade
balance for 1936 was unfavorable to Japan by one hundred
thirty four million yen but this was partly offset by a
favorable invisible trade balance (amount not yet determined)
Two. The above factors indicate a fairly strong finan-
cial position but the following unfavorable factors have
been weakening and will further weaken that position.

Three. The factors indicate the difficulties of the
government in regard to the price of bonds and the value
of the yen. There has been steady pressure on the yen
since the February 26 incident and this pressure bucame so
great in December last when coverage had to be found for
unusually large cotton purchases that the yen declined.
Thi s
weakness of the yen and the fear of the further pressure which
would result from expected large speculative imports in
anticipation of the projected increased tariff rates were
the causes of the foreign exchange control regulations :
announced and put into force on January 8 ( which are
tantamount to an import licensing system). The Finance
Ministry has explained that the exchange control regulations
will not be used to curtail necessary legitimate imports of
raw materials such as cotton and wool but are aimed against
speculative imports and are further designed to spread im-
ports over the year thus avoiding sudden heavy pressure on
the yen. The imposition of the regulations, however, clearly
shows the weakness of the yen and the scarcity of foreign
exchange stabilization funds.

Secretary of State
Washington
RUSH
5, Jan 1ry 13, noon, (SECTION TWO)
Four.
. he general opinion of competent observers
is that the financial situation in Japan is steadily grow-
ing worse being perhaps at this moment less sanguine than at
any time in Japan's recent history. This is chiefly because
of the abnormally large proposed budget for l937-38 which
is over 30% larger than the budget for the present fiscal
year and which with its inevitable additional burden of
deficit bonds is the principal cause of weakness in the
financial structure. This budget and succeeding budgets
which pr omise to be even larger will throw burdens upon
the nation faster than the increasing economic resources
can take care of the loads. Consequently observers
believe that the Government cannot escape difficulties in
maintaining the price of Government bonds and in sus-
taining the value of the yen at one shilling two pence.
The Yokohama Specie Bank has announced its intention of
holding the yen at this value largely it is believed
because of the fear of increased opposition abroad to
Japanese exports should the yen fall to new low levels.
At

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.98)
U -2- #5, Jan. 13, noon Sec 2 from Tokyo
At the same time it isbelieved that there is no pressing
danger of a financial collapse to Japan. The opinion pre-
vails that the government will take further artificial
measures much alcng the lines of the measures taken in
Germany to sustain the financial structure and especially to
maintain the pr'ice of its bonds and the value of the yen.
心.
It is believed that although the Government cannot avoid
a considerable measure of credit inflation acompanied by a
rapid rise in prices, it will succeed through such arti-
ficial measures in averting a dangerous crisis in the near
future.
Five. The foregoing is a general discussion of the
present financial situation. If the Treasury desires more
specific and detailed information the Embassy will en-
deavor to obtain the necessary data. (END MESSAGE)
GREW
